# Memre on Nicomedia (Syriac Version) — Mēmrē sur Nicomédie, version syriaque

**Author**: Éphrem de Nisibe  
**Series**: PO Tome 37  
**Columns**: pp. 16–308  

[p. 16] {#po.37.p16}
,ܨ ܙ × ܐ« &/ :^ ˆ ܐܳܘ ܦܳܪܽܘܩܳܐ ܕܡܶܢ ܣܰܝܒܽܘܬܳܐ ܨ« /9 ܠܳܐ ܬܶܣܒܰܪ ܕܪܰܚܺܝܩܳܐ ܗ̱ܝ ܡܰܘܬܳܐ܂ ܡܶܛܽܠ ܕܢܳܡܽܘܣܳܐ ܫܡܰܝܳܢܳܐ ܠܳܐ ܗ̱ܘܳܐ ܬܚܶܝܬ ܢܳܡܽܘܣܳܐ܂ ܩܶܨܬܳܐ ܐܺܝܬܰܘܗ̱ܝ ܨܶܒܝܳܢܶܗ
Oh! you who are far from old age, do not think that death is far away. For the heavenly Lawgiver is not subject to the law. The end of life is in his will; when he wants, he shortens and prolongs it. If he prolongs (it) for the old man, (the latter) is a child; if he shortens (it) for the child, (the latter) is an old man.
ܟܰܕ ܨܳܒܶܐ ܟܰܪܶܝ ܘܰܐܘܪܶܟ܂
He directs many with anger, he directs few with mercy. Where he wants to respect the order,


[p. 17] {#po.37.p17}
ܠܣܳܒܳܐ ܐܶܢ ܐܰܘܪܶܟ ܛܰܠܝܳܐ ܗ̱ܘ܂ ܠܛܰܠܝܳܐ ܐܶܢ ܟܰܪܶܝ ܣܳܒܳܐ ܗ̱ܘ܂ ܟܰܕ ܨܳܒܶܐ ܟܰܪܶܝ ܘܰܐܘܪܶܟ܂ ܟܰܕ ܨܳܒܶܐ ܟܰܪܶܝ ܘܰܐܘܪܶܟ܂ ܠܣܳܒܳܐ ܐܶܢ ܐܰܘܕܶܟ ܛܰܠܝܳܐ ܗ̱ܘ܂ ܠܛܰܠܝܳܐ ܐܶܢ ܟܰܪܶܝ ܣܳܒܳܐ ܗ̱ܘ܂ ܡܰܘܬܳܐ ܐܰܝܟܳܐ ܐܺܝܬܰܘܗ̱ܝ ܨܶܒܝܳܢܶܗ ܟܰܕ ܨܳܒܶܐ ܟܰܪܶܝ ܘܰܐܘܪܶܟ܂ ܠܣܳܒܳܐ ܐܶܢ ܐܰܘܪܶܟ ܛܰܠܝܳܐ ܗ̱ܘ܂ ܠܛܰܠܝܳܐ ܐܶܢ ܟܰܪܶܝ ܣܳܒܳܐ ܗ̱ܘ܂ ܡܶܛܽܠ ܕܢܳܡܽܘܣܳܐ ܫܡܰܝܳܢܳܐ ܠܳܐ ܗ̱ܘܳܐ ܬܚܶܝܬ ܢܳܡܽܘܣܳܐ܂ ܒܪܘܓܙܐ ܕܒܪ ܣܓܝ̈ܐܐ܂ ܒܪ̈ܚܡܐ ܕܒܪ ܕܠܝ̈ܠܐ܂ ܐܝܟܐ ܕܨܒ̇ܐ ܕܢܛܟܣ܂ ܣܒ̈ܐ ܐܙܠܝܢ ܩܕܡ ܫܒܪ̈ܐ܂ ܘܐܝܟܐ ܕܨܒܐ ܕܢܚܿܒܠ܂ ܟܠ ܡܘܫܢܝ̈ܢ ܕܒܪ ܫܒܛܗ܂ ܘܐܝܟܐ ܕܨܒܐ ܕܢܒܠܒܠ܂ ܫܒܪ̈ܐ ܐܙܠܝܢ ܩܕܡ ܣܒ̈ܐ܂ ܒܦܘܪ̈ܫܢܐ ܕܝܢ ܣܿܥܪ ܟܠ ܘܠܥܘܕܪ̈ܢܐ ܡܕܒܪ ܟܠ ܀
the old men go before the children. And where he wants to violate it, his rod directs all ages. And where he wants to overturn it, the children go before the old men. He operates everything with discernment and he does everything with a view to the good. He is merciful when he strikes and compassionate when he gives life.


[p. 18] {#po.37.p18}
ܪ̈ܚܡܐ ܐܝܬܘܗܝ ܟܕ ܢܓܕ ܘܚܢܢܐ ܟܕ ܝܗܒ ܚܝܐ܂ ܐܝܬ ܕܒܪܘܓܙܗ ܥܕܪ ܥܘܕܪܢܐ ܕܡܢܢ ܟܣܝܐ܂ ܣ ܥܘܕܪܢܗ ܕܝܢ ܠܐ ܟܣܝܐ ܡܛܠ ܕܪܝܚܐ ܕܥܘܕܪܢܗ ܢܦܩ܂ ܥܘܕܪܢܗ ܠܐ ܚܕܝܕ
Sometimes it is by his anger that he comes to help, with a help that is veiled to us. But his help is not hidden, for the perfume of his help exhales. His help is not limited, for on all sides there is help. The rod of his anger is a help, the tenderness of his love is an aid. 168-198. Murmur and blasphemy do not reach him.
ܡܛܠ ܕܡܢ ܟܠ ܓܒ̈ܝܢ ܥܘܕܪܢܐ܂ ܫܒܛܐ ܕܪܘܓܙܗ ܥܘܕܪܢܐ ܪܚܡܬܐ ܕܚܘܒܗ ܥܘܕܪܢܐ܂ !ܣ ܪܛܢܐ ܘܓܘܕܦܐ ܠܐ ܡܛܝܢ ܠܗ܂ ܛܘܒܘܗܝ ܠܗܘ ܕܡܨܐ ܘܠܐ ܪܛܢ ܥܠ ܙܕܝܩܘܬܗ܂ }! 9¡
Happy is he who, being able to, did not murmur against his justice. There can be neither murmur nor complaint for what he did and for what he does. It is not out of malice that he acts so that the mouth may murmur against him. It is not to harm that he acts, so that the tongue may murmur. It is not to fight injustice that he strikes; there is no one to blame his mercies. He is indeed above blame, for he is above malice.
ܠܐ ܪܛܢܐ ܐܝܬ ܘܠܐ ܬܘܟܬܐ ܥܠ ܡܕܡ ܕܥܒܕ ܘܥܠ ܡܕܡ ܕܥܒܕ܂ ܠܐ ܒܒܝܫܘܬܐ ܥܒܕ ܕܢܪܛܢ ܥܠܘܗܝ ܦܘܡܐ܂ ܠܐ ܠܚܒܠܐ ܥܒܕ ܕܬܪܛܢ ܠܫܢܐ܂ ܠܐ ܠܡܩܪܒܘ ܠܥܘܠܐ ܢܓܕ ܠܝܬ ܕܡܚܣܕ ܥܠ ܪ̈ܚܡܘܗܝ܂ ܡܛܠ ܕܠܥܠ ܡܢ ܚܣܕܐ ܡܛܠ ܕܠܥܠ ܡܢ ܒܝܫܘܬܐ܂ ܓܘܕܦܐ ܠܐ ܡܛܐ ܠܗ ܡܛܠ ܕܠܥܠ ܡܢ ܨܥܪܐ܂ ܐܢ ܠܫܢܝܢ ܟܦܪܝܢ ܒܗ ܠܫܢ̈ܐ ܕܥܝܪ̈ܐ ܡܘܕܝܢ ܠܗ܂ ܠܥܠ ܡܢ ܓܘܕܦܐ ܗ̱ܘ ܘܠܐ ܣܢܝܩ ܕܢܫܬܒܚ܂ ܟܝܢܗ ܒܗ ܩܐܡ ܠܐ ܪܒܐ ܘܠܐ ܙܥܘܪ܂ ܐܘܝ ܠܟ ܕܪܚܝܩ ܐܢܬ ܡܢ ܫܝܒܘܬܐ ܠܐ ܬܣܒܪ ܕܪܚܝܩ ܗܘ ܡܘܬܐ.
Blasphemy does not reach him, for he is above insult. If our tongues deny him, the tongues of the watchers give him thanks. He is above blasphemy, and he does not need to be glorified. (His) nature exists by itself; it does not increase nor does it diminish. If praise makes him greater, can blasphemy make him smaller? Praise does not make him greater; it is our mouths that are exalted by it. Blasphemy does not reach him by the fact that it is repeated by blasphemers. He lets it be believed that praise exalts him, so that we may praise and exalt him. He lets it be believed that he is saddened by our iniquity, so that we may not be impious and that we may be saved. 199-216. He strikes to amend.


[p. 19] {#po.37.p19}


[p. 20] {#po.37.p20}
ܡܛܠ ܕܢܡܘܣܐ ܫܡܝܢܐ ܠܐ ܡܫܬܥܒܕ ܠܢܡܘܣܐ. ܦܘܪܣܐ ܕܚܝ̈ܐ ܒܨܒܝܢܗ ܐܝܬܘܗܝ ܐܡܬܝ ܕܨܒܐ ܡܩܛܥ ܘܡܓܪܓ ܐܢ ܡܓܪܓ ܠܣܒܐ ܛܠܝܐ ܗܘ ܐܢ ܡܩܛܥ ܠܛܠܝܐ ܣܒܐ ܗܘ. ܣܓܝ̈ܐܐ ܒܪܘܓܙܐ ܡܕܒܪ ܙܥܘܪ̈ܐ ܒܪ̈ܚܡܐ ܡܕܒܪ. ܐܝܟܐ ܕܨܒܐ ܕܢܩܝܡ ܛܟܣܐ ܣܒ̈ܐ ܡܢ ܩܕܡ ܛܠܝ̈ܐ ܥܒܪܝܢ. ܘܐܝܟܐ ܕܨܒܐ ܕܢܫܪܝܘܗܝ ܚܘܛܪܗ ܠܟܠܗܘܢ ܫܢ̈ܐ ܡܕܒܪ. ܘܐܝܟܐ ܕܨܒܐ ܕܢܗܦܟܝܘܗܝ ܛܠܝ̈ܐ ܡܢ ܩܕܡ ܣܒ̈ܐ ܥܒܪܝܢ. ܟܠ ܡܕܡ ܒܦܘܪܫܢܐ ܥܒܕ
Sometimes he strikes the wicked in order to warn the good. And sometimes he strikes the righteous in order to inspire fear in the impious. For if it is said that the righteous were tormented, where will the impious hide? If he who is standing is struck, those who have fallen, thanks to him, leap and rise again. He strikes him who is standing in order to raise up, thanks to him, those who are overturned. He also strikes those who have fallen, so that he who is standing may not fall. Blessed is the Good One who comes to help, one by the other, both parties. On both (sides) it is good for us to threaten our person a little.


[p. 21] {#po.37.p21}
ܘܟܠ ܡܕܡ ܠܘܩܒܠ ܛܒܬܐ ܥܒܕ. ܡܪܚܡܢܐ ܗܘ ܐܡܬܝ ܕܡܚܐ ܘܚܢܢܐ ܗܘ ܐܡܬܝ ܕܝܗܒ ܚܝ̈ܐ. ܙܒܢܝܢ ܒܪܘܓܙܗ ܡܥܕܪ
It is good for us to threaten it, to put a brake on its avarice. 217-242. Wealth must be at the service of others.
ܥܘܕܪܢܐ ܕܡܟܣܝ ܡܢܢ. ܥܘܕܪܢܗ ܕܝܢ ܠܐ ܡܟܣܝ ܡܛܠ ܕܪܝܚܐ ܕܥܘܕܪܢܗ ܢܦܩ. ܥܘܕܪܢܗ ܠܐ ܡܚܕܕ ܡܛܠ ܕܡܢ ܟܠ ܓܒ̈ܝܢ ܐܝܬ ܥܘܕܪܢܐ. ܚܘܛܪܐ ܕܪܘܓܙܗ ܥܘܕܪܢܐ ܗܘ ܪܚܡܬ ܚܘܒܗ ܥܘܕܪܢܐ ܗܘ. ܪܛܢܐ ܘܓܘܕܦܐ ܠܐ ܡܛܝܢ ܠܗ. ܛܘܒܘܗܝ ܠܗܘ ܕܡܨܐ ܘܠܐ ܪܛܢ ܥܠ ܙܕܝܩܘܬܗ. ܠܐ ܐܝܬ ܪܛܢܐ ܘܠܐ ܬܘܪܥܡܐ ܥܠ ܡܕܡ ܕܥܒܕ ܘܥܠ ܡܕܡ ܕܥܒܕ. ܠܐ ܒܒܝܫܘܬܐ ܥܒܕ ܕܢܪܛܢ ܥܠܘܗܝ ܦܘܡܐ. ܠܐ ܠܚܒܠܐ ܥܒܕ ܕܬܪܛܢ ܠܫܢܐ.
Let us cut a little from our mouth, and give to the hungry mouths. For us, pleasures and voluptuousness, and for the poor, austerity. The sated gorge themselves without measure, but the hungry (do not have) even their measure. While we have just digested the first (dish), other dishes are being cooked. And in the evening, the wine has not yet been fermented, that by pouring pure wine one has already begun again. The canvas presses the weaver, but the vermin gnaw the previous ones. The poor are naked in the public squares, but the worm warms itself in your wool. The belly of the indigent is empty, but Mammon bursts the purses.


[p. 22] {#po.37.p22}
ܠܐ ܠܡܩܪܒܘ ܠܥܘܠܐ ܡܚܐ ܠܝܬ ܕܡܚܣܕ ܥܠ ܪ̈ܚܡܘܗܝ. ܡܛܠ ܕܠܥܠ ܡܢ ܚܣܕܐ ܗܘ ܡܛܠ ܕܠܥܠ ܡܢ ܒܝܫܘܬܐ ܗܘ. ܓܘܕܦܐ ܠܐ ܡܛܐ ܠܗ ܡܛܠ ܕܠܥܠ ܡܢ ܨܥܪܐ ܗܘ. ܐܢ ܠܫܢܝܢ ܟܦܪܝܢ ܒܗ ܠܫܢ̈ܐ ܕܥܝܪ̈ܐ ܡܘܕܝܢ ܠܗ. ܠܥܠ ܡܢ ܓܘܕܦܐ ܗܘ ܘܠܐ ܣܢܝܩ ܠܬܫܒܘܚܬܐ. ܟܝܢܗ ܒܩܢܘܡܗ ܐܝܬܘܗܝ
The body of the widow is withered, but the oil, in the vases, is rancid. If you make yourself the servant of all, you will resemble the Lord of all. Whoever resembles the Nourisher of the universe, that one is a providence for all. He could have, the Beneficent One who gave to you, given to your companion as to you. He enriched you, so that, like him, you might be a dispenser toward his servants. 243-272. To be wary of the Evil One.


[p. 23] {#po.37.p23}
ܠܐ ܪܒܐ ܘܠܐ ܙܥܘܪ. ܐܢ ܬܫܒܘܚܬܐ ܡܪܒܝܐ ܠܗ ܓܘܕܦܐ ܡܨܐ ܕܡܙܥܪ ܠܗ. ܬܫܒܘܚܬܐ ܠܐ ܡܪܒܝܐ ܠܗ
The Evil One is jealous of you: man of good, you could be his adversary. So that you are not his adversary, he has made you your own adversary.
ܐܠܐ ܦܘܡܝܢ ܡܬܪܝܡܝܢ ܒܗ. ܓܘܕܦܐ ܠܐ ܡܛܐ ܠܗ ܡܛܠ ܕܡܬܬܢܐ ܡܢ ܡܓܕܦܢ̈ܐ. ܫܒܩ ܕܢܣܒܪܘܢ ܕܬܫܒܘܚܬܐ ܡܪܝܡܐ ܠܗ ܕܢܫܒܚܝܘܗܝ ܘܢܪܝܡܝܘܗܝ. ܫܒܩ ܕܢܣܒܪܘܢ ܕܡܬܟܡܪ ܥܠ ܥܘܠܢ ܕܠܐ ܢܗܘܐ ܥܘܠ̈ܐ ܘܢܚܐ. ܡܚܐ ܕܢܬܬܩܢ. ܙܒܢܝܢ ܡܚܐ ܠܒܝܫ̈ܐ ܕܢܬܪܘܢ ܛܒ̈ܐ. ܘܙܒܢܝܢ ܡܚܐ ܠܙܕܝܩ̈ܐ ܕܢܕܚܠܘܢ ܪ̈ܫܝܥܐ.
The man with the evil eye is an image of the Evil One who hides. Whoever makes himself an imitator of God rises above humanity. The Evil One is jealous of your humanity, fearing that it might imitate divinity. Up to the garden, he promised you divinity. Where it was not permitted to become it, he promised you that you would be God. Where it is fitting and permitted to become it, it is here that he takes away life. May you guard yourself on two sides: as the Evil One he sins and harms without reason.


[p. 24] {#po.37.p24}
ܡܛܠ ܕܐܢ ܐܬܬܡܪ ܕܐܫܬܢܩܘ ܙܕܝܩ̈ܐ ܐܝܟܐ ܢܬܛܫܘܢ ܪ̈ܫܝܥܐ. ܐܢ ܡܚܐ ܠܩܝܡܐ ܢܦܘܠ̈ܐ ܒܗ ܩܦܨܝܢ ܘܩܝܡܝܢ. ܡܚܐ ܠܩܝܡܐ ܕܢܩܝܡ ܒܗ ܠܢܦܘܠ̈ܐ. £ ܐܘܝ ܠܕܪܚܝܩ ܐܢܬ ܡܢ ܣܝܒܘܬܐ ܠܐ ܬܣܒܪ ܕܪܚܝܩ ܡܘܬܐ ܕܠܝܬ ܠܗ ܠܢܡܘܣܐ ܕܢܡܘܣܐ ܕܠܥܠ
The magnificence that comes from God, he sows it in the midst of the rich. The mercy of God, he chases it from their midst. They imitated the magnificence of the Most High, they turned away from his sweetness. Fools! you have imitated on one point, add to it the imitation on the other point. What was not permitted, you have imitated, and what was permitted, you have turned away (from it). You act in the opposite way, like the Evil One who, in both cases, acts in the opposite way. 273-290. Understanding the warnings.
ܘܫܘܠܡܐ ܕܚܝܐ ܒܨܒܝܢܗ ܟܕ ܨܒܐ ܡܩܨܪ ܘܡܓܪܓ ܐܢ ܡܓܪܓ ܠܣܒܐ ܛܠܝܐ ܗܘ ܐܢ ܡܩܨܪ ܠܛܠܝܐ ܣܒܐ ܗܘ ܣܓܝܐܐ ܡܕܒܪ ܒܚܡܬܐ ܘܙܥܘܪܐ ܡܕܒܪ ܒܪܚܡܐ ܐܝܟܐ ܕܨܒܐ ܕܢܩܝܡ ܛܟܣܐ ܣܒܐ ܥܒܪܝܢ ܩܕܡ ܛܠܝܐ ܘܐܝܟܐ ܕܨܒܐ ܕܢܫܪܝܘܗܝ
Who would not be frightened by (these) warnings? Who would not tremble before (this) spectacle? Distant warnings are hard, present debts are grave. Merciful is indeed He who corrects: he sent the warning instead of the rod. If the warning corrects us, behold we are saved from the rod.


[p. 25] {#po.37.p25}
ܚܘܛܪܗ ܡܕܒܪ ܠܟܠ ܫܢܝܢ ܘܐܝܟܐ ܕܨܒܐ ܕܢܗܦܟܝܘܗܝ ܛܠܝܐ ܥܒܪܝܢ ܩܕܡ ܣܒܐ ܟܠ ܡܕܡ ܥܒܕ ܒܚܟܡܬܐ ܘܟܠ ܡܕܡ ܥܒܕ ܠܘܬ ܛܒܬܐ ܡܪܚܡ ܗܘ ܟܕ ܡܚܐ ܘܚܢܢ ܟܕ ܡܚܐ ܘܚܢܢ ܟܕ ܝܗܒ ܚܝܐ ܙܒܢܝܢ ܒܚܡܬܗ ܡܥܕܪ ܥܕܪܢܐ ܕܡܟܣܝ ܡܢܢ
If we neglect the warning, for us the rod does not pass by. Whoever refuses to listen to the warnings, the rod makes him leap unexpectedly. Thus these, who perished unexpectedly, refused to listen to the warnings and the "woe! woe!". They learned that provinces had been ravaged, and that cities had been overturned. They learned it, but despised the warnings, until the great bursting broke out. 291-324. The destruction of Nicomedia is a warning.
ܥܕܪܢܗ ܕܝܢ ܠܐ ܛܫܐ ܕܪܝܚܐ ܕܥܕܪܢܗ ܡܬܒܣܡ ܥܕܪܢܗ ܠܐ ܡܬܚܡ ܕܡܢ ܟܠ ܓܒܝܢ ܐܝܬ ܥܕܪܢܐ ܚܘܛܪܐ ܕܚܡܬܗ ܥܕܪܢܐ ܘܪܚܡܬܐ ܕܚܘܒܗ ܥܕܪܢܐ ܪܛܢܐ ܘܓܘܕܦܐ ܠܐ ܡܛܝܢ ܠܗ ܛܘܒܘܗܝ ܠܕܡܨܐ ܗܘܐ ܘܠܐ ܪܛܢ ܥܠ ܙܕܝܩܘܬܗ ܠܐ ܡܨܐ ܕܢܗܘܐ ܪܛܢܐ ܘܬܘܪܥܡܐ
The city, mother of kings, became mother of the suffocated. Whoever entered the city, entered royal dwellings. He enters new hells, he who enters it henceforth. Let us not be lazy like people who are far away, for, in all places, the wrath is near. Let us be frightened by the warning heard, for fear that in our country the event might occur.


[p. 26] {#po.37.p26}
ܥܠ ܡܕܡ ܕܥܒܕ ܘܥܠ ܡܕܡ ܕܥܒܕ ܠܐ ܗܘܐ ܒܒܝܫܘܬܐ ܥܒܕ ܕܢܪܛܢ ܥܠܘܗܝ ܦܘܡܐ ܠܐ ܗܘܐ ܠܚܒܠܐ ܥܒܕ ܕܬܪܛܢ ܠܫܢܐ ܠܐ ܗܘܐ ܠܡܩܪܒܘ ܥܘܠܐ ܡܚܐ ܠܝܬ ܕܡܚܣܕ ܥܠ ܪ̈ܚܡܘܗܝ ܕܠܥܠ ܗܘ ܡܢ ܚܣܕܐ ܕܠܥܠ ܗܘ ܡܢ ܒܝܫܘܬܐ ܓܘܕܦܐ ܠܐ ܡܛܐ ܠܗ ܕܠܥܠ ܗܘ ܡܢ ܨܥܪܐ
Penitence can oppose it so that it does not rush. Prayer can block the paths by which the wrath runs. From whatever place penitence calls, He who hears everything hears it. The city, a royal city, has become the sister of Sodom. While kings lived in its apartments, foxes live in its ditches. The city, queen of its companions, has become an object of terror for its companions.


[p. 27] {#po.37.p27}
ܐܢ ܠܫܢܝܢ ܟܦܪܝܢ ܒܗ ܠܫܢܝ̈ܢ ܕܥܝܪ̈ܐ ܡܘܕܝܢ ܠܗ
The traveler could not defend himself from entering it.
ܕܚܙܐ ܠܗ̇ ܗܫܐ ܕܚܠ ܠܡܚܙܝܘܬܗ̇ ܒܥܝܢ̈ܘܗܝ ܐܪܥܐ ܐܬܡܠܝܬ ܒܒܘܣܡܐ ܘܝܡܐ ܒܒ̈ܣܝܡܬܐ ܐܪܥܐ ܩܝܡܐ ܗܘܬ ܩܕܡܝܗ̇ ܐܝܟ ܥܒܕܐ ܒܟܠܗܝܢ ܒܘܣܡܝܗ̇ ܝܡܐ ܩܐܡ ܗܘܐ ܒܬܪܗ̇ ܝܡܐ ܘܐܪܥܐ ܡܫܡܫܝܢ ܗܘܘ ܠܗ̇ ܒܝܬ ܝܡܐ ܘܐܪܥܐ ܐܬܚܪܒܬ ܝܡܐ ܘܐܪܥܐ ܒܟܝܘ ܥܠܝܗ̇ ܠܝܬ ܕܢܣܒܪ ܒܛܝܒܘܬܐ ܘܢܬܪܦܐ ܡܢ ܬܝܒܘܬܐ ܙܕܝܩܘܬܗ ܘܛܝܒܘܬܗ ܕܐܠܗܐ ܐܦܢ ܚܪܒܗ ܠܗܕܐ ܡܕܝܢܬܐ ܛܒܐ ܗܘܐ ܘܙܕܝܩܐ
Whoever sees it now is afraid to contemplate it with his eyes. The earth was filled with delights, and the sea, with pleasures. The earth stood before it, like a servant, with all its enjoyments. The sea stood at its back; the sea and the earth served it. Between sea and earth it was destroyed. Sea and earth wept for it. No one can hope in grace, and be lazy regarding penitence. 327-338. Justice and goodness of God.


[p. 28] {#po.37.p28}
ܪ̈ܚܡܘܗܝ ܬܡܢ ܐܝܬ ܗܘܘ ܐܠܐ ܟܠܝ ܐܢܘܢ ܒܪܘܓܙܐ ܛܝܒܘܬܗ ܐܦ ܗܝ ܬܡܢ ܐܝܬ ܗܘܬ ܐܠܐ ܟܠܝ ܐܢܘܢ ܒܙܕܝܩܘܬܐ ܟܠܝ ܙܕܝܩܘܬܗ ܒܙܕܝܩ̈ܐ ܓܠܝ ܘܫܕܐ ܣܝܦܐ ܚܪܝܦܐ ܚܪܝܦܘܬܗ ܕܣܝܦܐ ܐܬܓܠܝܬ ܘܪ̈ܚܡܐ ܐܬܟܠܝܘ ܣܝܦܐ ܢܚܬ ܒܡܚܘܬܐ ܕܪܘܓܙܐ ܙܕܝܩ̈ܐ ܐܙܠܘ ܥܡ ܚܛܝ̈ܐ ܡܘܬܐ ܟܠ ܡܕܡ ܒܠܥ ܓܘܡܨܐ ܕܡܘܬܐ ܟܠ ܡܕܡ ܟܢܫ ܒܡܨܝܕܬܗ
Even if he destroyed this city, he was good and just. His mercy was there, but he held it back under the wrath. His grace also was there, but he held it back under the justice. He kept his justice hidden in the righteous. He laid bare (and) drew the sharp sword. The edge of the sword was laid bare, and the mercy was held back. The sword fell under the blow of the wrath. The righteous went away with the sinners. 339-432. Death has swallowed everything.


[p. 29] {#po.37.p29}
ܟܠ ܕܟܪ ܘܢܩܒܐ ܐܝܟܐ ܕܐܝܬܝܗܘܢ ܩܪܒ ܘܐܚܕ ܠܒܢܝܢ̈ܐ ܐܘܪ̈ܚܝܐ ܡܝܬܘ ܒܫܘܩ̈ܐ
The abyss of death enveloped everything in its nets, every male and female, wherever they were. It touched and seized the buildings. The travelers died in the public squares, and the sedentary in their dwellings.
ܘܥܡܪ̈ܝܐ ܒܡܕܝܪ̈ܝܗܘܢ ܢܘܟܪܝܐ ܥܠ ܘܥܡܪ ܕܠܐ ܨܒܝܢܐ ܒܕܘܟܬܐ ܪܘܪܒܬܐ ܕܠܐ ܐܓܪܐ ܠܝܬ ܕܡܦܩ ܠܗ ܡܢܗ̇ ܐܠܐ ܩܠܐ ܕܐܢܫܐ ܕܩܝܡܬܐ ܫܒܒ̈ܐ ܕܡܢ ܥܠܡ ܥܡܪܝܢ ܗܘܘ ܕܠܐ ܩܪܒܐ ܥܡܪܘ ܒܫܝܘܠ ܦܠܚ̈ܐ ܘܟܪ̈ܡܝܐ
A stranger entered, he lived unexpectedly in a vast space, without rent. There is no one to make him leave it, if it is not the voice of the resurrection. Neighbors who, always, had lived without quarrel, lived in the hells. Plowmen and vine-growers.


[p. 30] {#po.37.p30}
ܚܕ ܙܒܢ ܐܪܥܐ ܥܠ ܠܗ̇ ܘܐܫܟܚ ܒܗ̇ ܡܘܬܗ ܟܕ ܣܒܪ ܕܢܐܟܘܠ ܡܢܗ̇ ܐܟܠܬܗ ܗܝ ܕܠܥܦܪܐ ܐܬܦܢܝ ܟܪ̈ܡܝܐ ܡܝܬܘ ܒܟܪ̈ܡܝܗܘܢ ܘܓܢܢ̈ܝܐ ܒܓܢ̈ܢܝܗܘܢ ܕܚܠܬܐ ܐܩܝܡܬ ܐܢܘܢ ܘܣܓܝ̈ܐܐ ܡܝܬܘ ܡܢ ܙܘܥܐ ܪܘܓܙܐ ܩܛܦ ܕܠܐ ܨܒܝܢܐ ܩܛܦ̈ܐ ܘܩܛܘܦܝܗܘܢ ܡܒܪܟܢܐ ܠܐܪܥܐ ܠܛܗ̇
One had bought a land; he entered it and found his death there. While he hoped to eat of it, it ate him, for to the silt he returned. The vine-growers died in their vines, and the gardeners in their gardens. The fright made them surge, and many died from the earthquake. The wrath harvested unexpectedly, the harvests and their harvesters. He who blesses has cursed the earth: it has been devastated, it has become an object of terror. The cries so numerous, the wrath silenced them unexpectedly. The man presented himself before the Creator:
ܐܬܚܪܒܬ ܘܗܘܬ ܠܕܚܠܬܐ ܩ̈ܠܐ ܣܓܝ̈ܐܐ ܪܘܓܙܐ ܫܬܩ ܐܢܘܢ ܕܠܐ ܨܒܝܢܐ ܐܢܫܐ ܩܡ ܩܕܡ ܒܪܘܝܗ̇ ܬܘܒ ܩܪܐ ܠܗ ܠܩܝܡܬܐ ܘܥܦܪܐ ܟܣܝ ܫܘܦܪܗ ܟܠܝܐ ܟܠܝܗܘܢ ܠܐܣܝܪ̈ܐ ܘܪܘܓܙܐ ܦܬܚܗ̇ ܕܠܐ ܫܐܠ
Again He calls him to the resurrection, and the dust covers his finery. Prisoners and thieves.


[p. 31] {#po.37.p31}
ܕܡܢ ܙܒܢܐ ܣܓܝܐܐ ܟܠܝܢ ܗܘܘ ܬܡܢ ܘܕܝܢ̈ܐ ܠܐ ܕܝܢܝܢ ܗܘܘ ܠܗܘܢ ܢܚܬ ܕܝܢܐ ܕܕܝܢ̈ܐ ܘܕܢ ܥܠܬܗܘܢ ܒܚܕܐ ܫܥܬܐ ܡܢܗܘܢ ܟܕ ܡܬܟܬܫܝܢ ܗܘܘ ܒܝܢܬܗܘܢ
The prison held the prisoners locked up; wrath opened it without asking anything. For they had been held there for a long time, and the judges were not judging them. The Judge of judges descended; He judged their cause in an instant.


[p. 32] {#po.37.p32}
ܡܘܬܐ ܫܪܝ ܠܟܬܝܫܬܗܘܢ
As for some, while they were quarreling among themselves, death settled the quarrels.
ܚܕ ܟܕ ܝܗܒ ܡܘܗܒ̈ܬܐ ܘܐܚܪܢܐ ܟܕ ܟܢܫ ܟܢ̈ܫܐ ܚܕ ܡܢ ܩܢܝܢܗ ܝܗܒ ܠܩܪܝܒܗ ܘܐܚܪܢܐ ܚܛܦ ܕܚܒܪ̈ܘܗܝ ܐܙܠ ܪܫܝܥܐ ܟܕ ܥܒܕ ܥܘܠܐ ܐܙܠ ܙܕܝܩܐ ܟܕ ܥܒܕ ܙܕܝܩܘܬܐ ܒܢ̈ܝܐ ܘܟܢ̈ܫܐ ܚܕ ܟܕ ܫܕܐ ܫܬܐܣܬܐ
One while he was giving gifts, another while he was hoarding ingots. One was giving his goods to his neighbor, and the other was snatching those of (his) neighbors. The impious one departed while he was committing iniquity; the just one departed while he was practicing justice. Builders and hoarders.
ܢܦܠ ܒܢܝܢܐ ܕܒܢܐ ܘܐܚܪܢܐ ܒܢܐ ܒܝܬܐ ܘܨܒܬܗ ܘܐܬܩܢܗ ܘܓܗܢܐ ܗܘܐ ܒܝܬܗ ܐܚܪܢܐ ܕܡܢ ܛܠܝܘܬܗ ܠܐܝ ܕܢܬܩܢ ܣܝܡ̈ܬܐ ܠܣܝܒܘܬܗ ܡܘܬܐ ܡܢ ܠܐ ܡܬܚܫܒܢܘܬܐ ܦܪܩܗ ܐܝܟ ܥܒܕܐ ܡܢ ܨܥܪ̈ܘܗܝ ܚܕ ܡܬܚܫܒ ܗܘܐ ܥܠ ܝܘܡܗ ܘܐܚܪܢܐ ܡܘܪܟ ܗܘܐ ܨܥܪ̈ܘܗܝ ܥܠܘܠ̈ܐ ܘܙܠܝܠ̈ܐ
One, while he was laying the foundations (of a house), (saw) the one he had just built collapse. Another made a house, adorned it, and furnished it, and hell was his house. Another, who had labored since (his) youth to build up reserves for his old age, death, unexpectedly, freed him, like a slave, from (his) worries. One was reflecting on his day, and the other was prolonging his worries. Misers and gluttons.


[p. 33] {#po.37.p33}
ܠܘܬ ܦܘܡ̈ܐ ܫܬܝܩ̈ܐ ܟܢܝܫܝܢ ܣܝܡ̈ܬܐ ܕܥܠܘܠܘܬܐ ܣܡܬ ܠܗ̇ ܚܕ ܢܛܪ ܗܘܐ ܚܡܪܗ ܘܐܚܪܢܐ ܡܥܬܩ ܗܘܐ ܥܠܠܬܗ ܡܐܟܘܠ̈ܬܐ ܠܙܠܝܠܘܬܗ ܟܢܝܫܢ ܗܘܝ ܠܘܬܗ ܠܓܗܢܐ ܚܕ ܠܚܡܐ ܠܟܦܢ̈ܐ ܠܐ ܝܗܒ ܘܐܬܩܒܪ ܥܡܗ ܒܢܦܩܬܗ ܘܐܚܪܢܐ ܟܕ ܦܪܫ ܦܬܘܪܐ ܐܬܬܚܕ ܦܘܡܗ ܥܠ ܡܐܟܘܠ̈ܬܐ
Near silent mouths are amassed the reserves that avarice was reserving for itself. One was watching over his wine, and the other was letting his harvest age. Provisions for his gluttony were amassed near him, for hell! One was refusing bread to the hungry, and he was buried with it in his expense. As for the other, while he was setting the table, his mouth closed before the dishes. Lenders and borrowers.


[p. 34] {#po.37.p34}
The lender and the borrower were consuming themselves in great worries.
ܡܘܙܦܢܐ ܘܠܘܝܐ ܡܬܬܚܝܠܝܢ ܗܘܘ ܒܨܥܪ̈ܐ ܪ̈ܘܪܒܐ܂ ܨܥܪܐ ܕܚܢܩ ܠܣܓܝܐܐ ܚܢܩܗ ܪܘܓܙܐ܂ ܚܕ ܟܕ ܬܩܠ ܟܣܦܗ
The worry that suffocated many, wrath has suffocated it. One, while he was weighing his money, descended alive into hell. The other, while he was taking an oath, departed with the oath on his mouth. Those who fast, eaters, prostitutes, and judges.


[p. 35] {#po.37.p35}
ܠܫܝܘܠ ܢܚܬ ܚܝܐ܂ ܘܐܚܪܢܐ ܟܕ ܝܡܐ ܡܘܡܬܐ ܐܙܠ ܘܡܘܡܬܐ ܒܦܘܡܗ܂ ܨܝܡܐ ܘܐܟܘܠܐ ܘܙܢܝܬܐ ܘܕܝܢܐ܂ ܨܝܡܐ ܐܙܠ ܒܨܘܡܗ ܘܐܟܘܠܐ ܒܐܟܠܗ܂ ܙܢܝܬܐ ܐܙܠܬ ܒܛܢܦܘܬܗ ܘܕܟܝܬܐ ܒܕܟܝܘܬܗ܂
The faster went away during his fast, and the glutton in his orgy. The prostitute went away in her impurity, and the pure one in her purity. The judges died at the tribunal, bribes still in their hands. Their debates were extinguished unexpectedly, their thefts still in their belts. Married women or those living in continence.
ܕܝܢܐ ܡܝܬܘ ܒܕܝܢܐ ܘܫܘܚܕܐ ܒܐܝܕܝܗܘܢ܂ ܡܠܬܐ ܕܡܬܚܪܝܢ ܗܘܘ ܫܠܝܬ ܘܓܢܒܬܐ ܒܚܨܝܗܘܢ܂ ܢܫܐ ܕܒܥܠܝܗܝܢ ܘܕܒܒܬܘܠܘܬܐ܂ ܟܠܬܐ ܐܙܠܬ ܒܬܘܠܝܬܗ ܘܒܬܘܠܬܐ ܒܩܠܝܬܗ܂ ܚܕܐ ܝܬܒܬ ܥܠ ܣܩܐ ܘܐܚܪܬܐ ܥܠ ܐܣܟܝܡܐ܂ ܚܕܐ ܫܩܠܬ ܥܡܗ ܣܘܥܪ̈ܢܗ ܒܝ̈ܫܐ ܘܐܚܪܬܐ ܙܘܕܐ ܛܒܐ܂ ܙܕܝܩܐ ܡܬܛܝܒ ܕܢܦܪܫ ܥܒܕܝܗܝܢ ܘܣܘܥܪ̈ܢܝܗܝܢ܂ ܠܘܩܒܠ ܓܝܪ ܝܕܥܬܗ ܕܟܠܝܕܥ
Young brides disappeared with their nuptial chambers, and chaste women with their cells. One was sitting on a hairshirt, and the other on a cloak. One took with her her evil deeds, and the other, on the contrary, an excellent viaticum. The Just One prepares to discern their labors and their works. For from the knowledge of the Omniscient nothing can escape. When they were alive and debauched, their works did not escape Him. And now that they are dead and confounded, their conduct does not escape Him.

### FOURTH (MEMRA) ON NICOMEDIA
ܠܐ ܡܫܟܚ ܡܕܡ ܕܢܥܪܘܩ܂
1-32. The power of penance.


[p. 36] {#po.37.p36}


[p. 37] {#po.37.p37}
ܟܕ ܚܝ̈ܢ ܗܘܝ ܘܡܫܬܥܠܝܢ ܠܐ ܥܪܩܘ ܡܢܗ ܣܘܥܪ̈ܢܝܗܝܢ܂ ܘܗܫܐ ܟܕ ܡܝ̈ܬܢ ܘܡܬܒܠܒܠܢ ܠܐ ܥܪܩ ܡܢܗܘܢ ܕܘܒܪ̈ܝܗܝܢ܂ ܡܐܡܪܐ ܪܒܝܥܝܐ ܥܠ ܢܝܩܘܡܝܕܝܐ ܚܝܠܐ ܕܬܝܒܘܬܐ܂ ܢܬܬܥܝܩܘܢ ܬܝܒܐ ܪ̈ܚܡܝ ܬܝܒܘܬܐ܂ ܪܘܓܙܐ ܓܝܪ ܒܥܕܢܐ ܕܦܘܪܩܢܐ ܐܚܕ ܬܪܥܐ ܠܘܩܒܠܗ܂ ܠܐ ܥܠܬ ܐܝܟ ܥܝܕܐ ܠܕܝܢܐ ܠܘܩܒܠ ܕܝܢܐ ܕܬܒܛܠ ܒܣܩܐ ܘܒܩܛܡܐ
Let penitents, friends of penance, go into mourning: wrath, at the hour of salvation, indeed closed the door before it. It did not enter, according to custom, into the tribunal, in the presence of the Judge, in order to have the sentence concerning the cities annulled, thanks to sackcloth and ashes. Very eloquent at the tribunal is penance in the presence of the Judge. With numerous gifts and presents, it enters and snatches deliverance. The King is quite amiable when it stands before His majesty, spreading its sackcloth and scattering ashes, bowing its head and dark in complexion.
ܕܝܢܐ ܕܥܠ ܡܕܝܢܬܐ܂ ܡܠܝܠܬܐ ܗܝ ܬܝܒܘܬܐ ܒܕܝܢܐ ܠܘܩܒܠ ܕܝܢܐ܂ ܒܡܘܗܒ̈ܬܐ ܣܓܝ̈ܐܬܐ ܘܒܫܘܠܡ̈ܢܐ ܥܐܠܐ ܘܚܛܦܐ ܦܘܪܩܢܐ܂ ܡܠܟܐ ܚܒܝܒܐ ܗܝ ܟܠܗ ܟܕ ܩܝܡܐ ܠܘܩܒܠ ܪܒܘܬܗ ܦܪܝܣܐ ܣܩܗ ܘܡܒܕܪܐ ܩܛܡܐ ܟܦܝܦܐ ܪܝܫܗ ܘܥܟܝܪܐ ܦܪܨܘܦܗ܂ ܒܘܪ̈ܟܝܗ ܟܦܝܦܢ ܠܨܠܘܬܐ ܘܐܝܕܝܗ ܡܫܠܒܢ ܠܒܥܘܬܐ܂ ܩܠܐ ܡܪܝܪܐ ܡܢ ܦܘܡܗ
Its knees are bent for prayer, its hands are joined for the request. A plaintive cry (comes) from its mouth, abundant tears from its eyes. When it sees it, wrath moves away; (penance) triumphs over it through great importunity. The naked sword, if it sees it, returns to its sheath (and) hides. It does not doubt mercy; it appeases its adversary.


[p. 38] {#po.37.p38}
ܘܕܡܥ̈ܐ ܣܓܝ̈ܐܬܐ ܡܢ ܥܝܢܝܗ܂ ܟܕ ܚܙܝܗ ܪܘܓܙܐ ܪܚܩ
Its love has obtained mercy; its sacrifice has obtained justice.


[p. 39] {#po.37.p39}
ܘܙܟܬܗ ܒܥܠܝܨܘܬܐ ܪܒܬܐ܂ ܣܝܦܐ ܓܠܝܐ ܐܢ ܚܙܝܗ ܥܠ ܠܚܪܒܗ ܘܐܬܛܫܝ܂ ܠܐ ܡܬܦܠܓܐ ܥܠ ܪ̈ܚܡܐ ܘܡܫܝܢܐ ܠܩܪܝܒܗ܂ ܚܘܒܗ ܪ̈ܚܡܐ ܫܩܠ ܘܕܒܚܗ ܙܕܝܩܘܬܐ܂
Because the one who supplicates is thus importunate, it has obtained two things for the one who humbles himself: its supplication has obtained grace, its prayer has obtained justice. 33-54. Hypocritical penance.
ܐܬܬ ܨܬ & ¦ ܬܬܐ ܐܬܐܬ ܢܐܐ ܐ ܬܐܐ ܐ ܐ ܬ ܬܐ ܐܨ ܐܬܕ × :ܨ ܹ ܹܨ ܕܠܐ ܐܩܘܣ ܬܘܐܣ ܛܘ ܖ ܐܬ ܐܬܐܢ ܐܨ ܬ :ܨܐ ܬ ܬܐ ^ »ܨ ܐܙ
The penance of this age is all in hypocrisies. They always make themselves penitents; they always make themselves sinners. It is only at the last hour that they call upon penance to save them. Thus the child, when he is mistreated, takes refuge near whoever can reassure him; but when his tutor is silent, he no longer cares about his defender. Those who choose (the times) resemble children; those who practice (it) with moderation, impudent ones.


[p. 40] {#po.37.p40}
ܐܬ ,ܠܘܬ ܙܙܐ:« ,88ܦ ܬܘܐ« ܐ ,}ܐ =« ܵܐ ܬܬ ܐ :ܘ :ܐܬܐܕ ܨܐܬ ܐ ܐܬܐܬ ,ܬܐܨ ܐ ܙܬܬ ܐ ܐ ܕ ܐܨ 6887:8ܛܛ ܐ ܬܨ :ܬܐܨ < ܐܐ ܬܐ ܕܙܐ
In wrath, they call a defender; in peace, they no longer care about him. When they see wrath coming, they take refuge in penance. When they see the rod rising, they take off their clothes and put on hairshirts. When they see the rod lowered, they strip off the hairshirts and put on sin. They are clever, but impious. They are not inferior to Satan! 55-94. Power and freedom.


[p. 41] {#po.37.p41}
ܐܐܬ ܐܬܬ ܐ ܢܐ ܢ ܬܐܨܘ ܬ ܨܐܐ ܬܗ ܘܬܨܙ‫ ܙܐ ܐܬ :ܐܐܬ ܐܨ ܐܬ ܨܐ ܐܠܬ
While they are stronger than the Evil One, they are defeated by him in the combat. Does delectation insinuate itself openly? It steals the strength of our freedom. When a passion increases a little,
ܐܬ ܬ ܕܐܨ« × :× :»× ܨܐ ܐܗ ܐܐܐ :ܐܗܢ ܨܐܐ ܕܐܐܐ¡ ܠ ܐܐܐ '
the strength (of freedom) shows great courage. As long as it has not been felt, freedom shows that its strength is hidden within itself. Wrath appears to it like a furnace; it examines its power and nature.
ܨ & ,6« ܠ ܬ »ܩ ܬ ݇ܐ« ܐ ܘ ܘܬܐ ܐܨܘ ܐܬܙ ܘܨܬ ܢܐܬ ܐܬܘܨܡ ܬܐܐ »8 ܘܐ ܐܬܐ ܐܐ / ܐܬܙ ܐܐܬ« ܙ ܬܐܨ ܐ ܬً »ܡ ܐ ܐܐ ܬ ܟ ܢܐܘ ,)ܐܧ ܐ ܐܬ ܬܐ ܬܐܨܘ ,)ܨ ܬ/ܬً0 ܐܬܐܐ ܐܙܘܙ ܐ ܙ <
If wrath is violent, violent in it is its strength. If wrath is weak, moderate in it is its strength. In the presence of wrath, as in combat, it tramples those who trampled it. Pride is humbled in wrath, and arrogance is lowered. The anxiety that consumed many, the anxiety of punishment chases it away.


[p. 42] {#po.37.p42}
ܩܬ ܬܐ ܟ ܧ&ܐܨ ܳܬ ܗ ܐ 7/ ܕ ܐܐ ܙ ܢܐܐ ܐ ܬܬܢܐ ܙ ܘ ܐܐ :ܐܧ ܘ ܨܐܐ ܠܐܬ ܨ
The ardor of concupiscence is extinguished, the audacity of concupiscence calms down. The belly is defeated by fasting, the body adorns itself with sackcloth. Upon itself, freedom has put on armor in oppression. How lazy it is in peace, courageous in wrath! Numerous are indeed the feats of freedom.


[p. 43] {#po.37.p43}
ܐ ܪ ܟ ܣ ܠܬܐ ܙܘ ܩܙ ܐܨܢܘ ܐ :ܐܠܨܨ ܨܐܬ ܘ ܐܬ¿ ܘ ` :+ ܘ ܬܠܐ ܠܐܢܵܐ/ ܘܰܘܨܐ ܬܘܨ
When it comes to be oppressed, let us act with speed and, like guilty ones, let us hasten. Whoever takes the lead of wrath gives proof of freedom. He then gives the pledge of it, while waiting to give proof of it. As long as its power is thwarted, the will is reprimanded; for while it can deploy its strength,


[p. 44] {#po.37.p44}
ܬܬܐ } =« ܐܧ /¶ ܐܬܬ ܨܧ ܢܐܘ ܕ“ ×
it does not want to show courage. 95-114. Wrath and hypocritical penance.
ܪܘܓܙܐ ܥܠܬ ܥܡ ܙܕܝܩܘܬܐ ܘܒܕܝܢܐ ܟܐܬ ܠܚܛܝܐ. ܐܢ ܐܝܬ ܚܛܝܐ ܕܚܟܝܡܝܢ ܐܢܘܢ
Wrath entered with justice and, at the tribunal, reprimanded the wicked. If there are sinners who are clever, there are also penitents who are hypocritical. When wrath falls upon them, they take refuge in penance. And when wrath turns away and goes away, they return to their iniquities. They have made their penance a temporary shelter.


[p. 45] {#po.37.p45}
ܐܝܬ ܐܦ ܬܝܒܐ ܕܢܟܝܠܝܢ ܐܢܘܢ. ܟܕ ܢܦܠ ܥܠܝܗܘܢ ܪܘܓܙܐ ܥܪܩܝܢ ܠܘܬ ܬܝܒܘܬܐ. ܘܟܕ ܦܢܐ ܪܘܓܙܐ ܘܐܙܠ ܗܢܘܢ ܦܢܘ ܠܘܬ ܥܘܠܝܗܘܢ. ܥܒܕܘ ܠܬܝܒܘܬܗܘܢ ܡܛܫܝܬܐ ܕܥܒܪܐ.
Wrath has called our penance a refuge of hypocrisy! But the insult (done) to penance is a fine eulogy for it. Indeed, it is revealed in the blame, for the virtuous (penance) receives its eulogy there. If indeed the hypocritical (penance) of penitents can become a shelter, then the true (penance) of those who convert, how much more will it be a refuge! 115-150. Penance could bend justice.


[p. 46] {#po.37.p46}
ܩܪܐ ܪܘܓܙܐ ܠܬܝܒܘܬܢ ܡܛܫܝܬܐ ܕܢܟܠܐ. ܐܠܐ ܨܥܪܐ ܕܬܝܒܘܬܐ ܫܘܒܚܐ ܗܘ ܠܗ̇ ܝܐܐ. ܡܛܠ ܕܒܨܥܪܐ ܡܬܓܠܝܐ
Wrath has begged, insisted, that the Judge let it act. Indeed, since penance is not vigilant, it can destroy the city unexpectedly. (Wrath) suspected the strength (of penance), for it knew what its insistence is. Let it arrive, indeed, dressed in a hairshirt, the sword returns to its sheath!
ܕܬܡܢ ܡܫܬܒܚܐ ܬܝܒܘܬܐ ܫܪܝܪܬܐ. ܐܢ ܓܝܪ ܬܝܒܘܬܐ ܢܟܝܠܬܐ ܕܬܝܒܐ
But justice itself has honored penance, for it did not reveal to penance
ܡܛܫܝܬܐ ܡܫܟܚܐ ܕܬܗܘܐ ܟܡܐ ܝܬܝܪܐܝܬ ܬܝܒܘܬܐ ܫܪܝܪܬܐ ܕܡܬܦܢܝܢܐ ܡܛܫܝܬܐ ܬܗܘܐ. ܬܝܒܘܬܐ ܡܫܟܚܐ ܕܬܟܦܝܘܗܝ ܠܙܕܝܩܘܬܐ.
the moment when it went out to destroy. It is out of honor for penance that justice did this, so as not to incur (its) vindictiveness, and that it, the illustrious one, might not despise the afflicted one.


[p. 47] {#po.37.p47}
ܪܘܓܙܐ ܒܥܐ ܘܐܠܨ ܕܢܫܒܩܝܘܗܝ ܕܝܢܐ ܕܢܥܒܕ. ܡܛܠ ܕܬܝܒܘܬܐ ܠܐ ܚܪܝܦܐ ܡܫܟܚܐ ܕܬܚܪܒ ܡܕܝܢܬܐ ܡܢ ܫܠܝܐ.
Penance would have rushed toward justice, with supplications; and justice would have put it in the midst of danger and destruction. If it had despised penance, there would have been an outrage to penance; and if it had accepted its request, there would have been danger for our laziness. Justice turned away out of honor for penance, in order to show that the pressure of the latter can turn away wrath through its cries.


[p. 48] {#po.37.p48}
ܪܘܓܙܐ ܚܫܕ ܒܚܝܠܗ̇ ܡܛܠ ܕܝܕܥ ܗܘܐ ܠܐܠܨܘܬܗ̇. ܕܐܢ ܬܝܬܐ ܒܣܩܐ ܠܒܝܫܐ ܣܝܦܐ ܥܐܠ ܠܚܪܬܗ. ܐܠܐ ܙܕܝܩܘܬܐ ܓܝܪ ܝܩܪܬ ܠܬܝܒܘܬܐ ܕܠܐ ܓܠܝܬ ܠܬܝܒܘܬܐ ܥܕܢܐ ܕܢܦܩܬ ܠܚܒܠܐ.
One half of the city was indeed overturned and, over it, the other half lamented. Penance let out a cry unexpectedly, and wrath fell unexpectedly. It gave us a double proof: (its) valor and (its) overwhelming grief. Patience and anxiety, it made (them) enter and placed them before (wrath). 151-186. Wrath does not wait for penance.
ܒܡܘܬܪܢܘܬܐ ܕܪܘܓܙܐ ܐܝܬ ܣܒܪܐ ܕܬܐܬܐ ܦܘܪܣܐ ܕܬܝܒܘܬܐ ܘܒܥܓܠܘܬܗ ܚܣܝܪ ܣܒܪܐ ܕܠܐ ܢܗܘܐ ܦܘܪܣܐ ܕܣܒܪܐ. ܦܘܪܣܐ ܩܐܡ ܒܡܨܥܬ ܪܘܓܙܐ ܕܬܚܘܐ ܬܝܒܘܬܐ ܚܝܠܐ.
In a prolongation of anger, there is hope that an occasion for penitence may come, and in its swiftness, despair that there is no longer an occasion to have hope. A circumstance presents itself in the midst of anger so that penitence may show its valor. Blows arrive unexpectedly so that sloth may not use deceit.


[p. 49] {#po.37.p49}
ܡܚܘܬܐ ܡܛܝܢ ܕܠܐ ܣܒܪܐ ܕܠܐ ܬܬܢܟܠ ܚܒܝܠܘܬܐ.
A slow anger makes penitence our refuge. Swift, it removes a recourse to which hope clings, which departs with the anger.
ܪܘܓܙܐ ܡܘܬܪܢܐ ܬܝܒܘܬܐ ܥܒܕ ܠܢ ܡܪܥܝܬܐ. ܥܓܠܝܐ ܫܩܠ ܡܢܢ ܡܪܥܝܬܐ ܕܐܚܝܕ ܒܗ ܣܒܪܐ ܕܐܙܠ ܥܡ ܪܘܓܙܐ.
But after anger, it is fitting to be penitent.
ܐܠܐ ܒܬܪ ܪܘܓܙܐ ܙܕܩ ܕܢܗܘܐ ܬܝܒܐ. ܢܗܘܐ ܚܪܝܨܝܢ ܘܡܛܝܒܝܢ ܕܐܝܬ ܪܘܓܙܐ ܕܚܪܒ ܕܠܐ ܣܒܪܐ. ܚܪܒ ܡܕܝܢܬܐ ܕܠܐ ܣܒܪܐ ܕܠܐ ܢܛܪ ܬܝܒܘܬܐ. ܘܐܦ ܡܕܝܢܬܐ ܫܩܠ ܒܚܕ ܪܦܦܐ ܕܠܐ ܢܛܪ ܣܩܐ ܘܩܛܡܐ. ܒܬܐ ܫܩܠ ܥܡ ܥܡܪܝܗܘܢ ܕܠܐ ܢܛܪ ܨܘܡܗܘܢ. ܪܘܓܙܐ ܫܩܠ ܐܣܛܘܐܬܐ
Let us be vigilant and ready, for there is an anger that devastates unexpectedly. It ravaged provinces unexpectedly without waiting for penitence. It also overturned cities in an instant, without waiting for sackcloth and ashes. It struck down houses with their inhabitants, without waiting for their fasting. Anger overturned porticoes, without being patient or waiting for years.


[p. 50] {#po.37.p50}
ܕܠܐ ܢܓܪ ܘܢܛܪ ܫܢܝܐ. ܙܕܝܩܐ ܕܐܫܟܚ ܬܡܢ ܕܐܝܬܘܗܝ ܛܝܒܐ ܕܒܪܗ. ܢܟܝܠܐ ܫܩܠ ܕܠܐ ܣܒܪܐ ܕܡܬܬܟܠ ܗܘܐ ܥܠ ܬܝܒܘܬܐ. ܕܥܡܗ ܗܘܐ ܙܘܕܗ ܐܙܠ ܒܣܒܪܐ ܪܒܐ. ܕܠܐ ܐܚܝܕ ܗܘܐ ܒܗ ܐܠܐ ܒܣܒܪܐ ܣܒܪܗ ܐܬܦܣܩ ܕܠܐ ܣܒܪܐ. ܙܘܥܐ ܐܬܓܠܝ ܘܫܩܠ ܫܘܪܐ ܕܠܐ ܢܛܪ ܨܠܘܬܐ. ܫܩܠ ܘܫܕܐ ܡܛܠܠܐ ܕܠܐ ܢܛܪ ܒܥܘܬܐ.
The just man it found there, it took him, since he was ready. The hypocrite, it struck down unexpectedly, he who was counting on penitence. He whose viaticum was with him departed with great hope. He who was attached to it only in hope, his hope was cut off unexpectedly. The earthquake broke out and demolished the ramparts without waiting for prayer. It demolished and struck down the roofs without waiting for supplications. 187-218. The necessary limits of knowledge.


[p. 51] {#po.37.p51}
ܬܚܘܡܐ ܕܝܕܥܬܐ ܕܡܬܒܥܝܢ. ܠܝܕܘܥ ܟܠܗܝܢ ܙܕܩ ܠܝܕܘܥ ܟܠܗܝܢ ܒܠܚܘܕ. ܒܪ̈ܝܬܐ ܘܓܒܝܠܬܐ ܘܥܒܝܕܬܐ ܠܐ ܩܒܠܬ ܐܠܐ ܝܕܥܬܐ ܬܚܘܡܝܬܐ.
It is to the Omniscient alone that it is fitting to know all things; created, fashioned, and made beings have received only limited knowledge. God, who knows everything, has restricted our knowledge in some way.


[p. 52] {#po.37.p52}
ܐܠܗܐ ܕܝܕܥ ܟܠ ܚܒܫ ܒܡܕܡ ܝܕܥܬܢ.
Knowledge, which is generally good, can be harmful in some point.
ܝܕܥܬܐ ܕܒܓܠܝܐ ܛܒܐ ܗܝ ܒܡܕܡ ܡܙܩܐ. ܐܢܫ ܠܐ ܝܕܥ ܝܘܡܐ ܕܡܘܬܗ ܘܐܢ ܝܕܥ ܗܘܐ ܡܙܩ ܗܘܐ. ܕܚܝܘܗܝ ܙܥܘܪܝܢ ܚܠܫ ܗܘܐ ܘܕܚܝܘܗܝ ܣܓܝܐܝܢ ܡܬܪܫܠ ܗܘܐ.
No one knows the day of his death, and if he knew it, it would be harmful. He whose life would be short would wither away, and he whose life would be long would be slothful. Many damages would result from this premature knowledge.
ܣܓܝܐܝܢ ܚܘܣܪ̈ܢܐ ܡܬܝܠܕܝܢ ܡܢ ܗܕܐ ܝܕܥܬܐ ܩܕܡܝܬܐ. ܐܘ ܪܫܠܘܬܐ ܡܕܡܟܐ ܠܢ ܐܘ ܥܩܬܐ ܡܫܪܝܐ ܠܢ. ܠܙܕܝܩܐ ܗܕܐ ܝܕܥܬܐ ܡܙܩܐ ܒܩܪܒܐ. ܩܫܐ ܗܘܐ ܠܗ ܠܡܣܝܒܪܘ ܝܘܩܪܗ ܟܕ ܚܙܐ ܚܝܐ ܣܓܝܐܐ. ܬܘܫܦܚܬܐ ܗܝ ܠܙܕܝܩܐ ܕܠܐ ܝܕܥ ܡܫܘܚܬܐ ܕܫܢܘܗܝ. ܕܐܝܟ ܕܚܫܒ ܕܙܥܘܪܝܢ ܚܝܘܗܝ
Either sloth would put us to sleep, or sadness would paralyze us. For the just man, this knowledge would be harmful in the struggle. It would be difficult for him to bear the weight of it when he saw a long life. It is a consolation for the just man not to know the measure of his years. As he estimates that he will live little, he runs for a long time without tiring.


[p. 53] {#po.37.p53}
ܪܗܛ ܣܓܝܐܐ ܕܠܐ ܥܝܦܘܬܐ. ܠܚܨܝܦܐ ܡܙܩܐ ܗܘܐ ܘܚܨܝܦܐ ܕܢܕܥ ܙܒܢܐ ܕܫܘܠܡܐ ܕܚܝܘܗܝ. ܐܢ ܝܕܥ ܗܘܐ ܕܣܓܝܐܝܢ ܡܬܚܢܦ ܗܘܐ ܘܐܢ ܝܕܥ ܗܘܐ ܕܙܥܘܪܝܢ ܡܬܪܛܢ ܗܘܐ. ܣܟܢܬܐ ܪܒܬܐ ܗܘܬ ܠܒܪܢܫܐ ܕܢܕܥ ܡܢܝܢܐ ܕܝܘܡܘܗܝ. ܡܦܣܩ ܗܘܐ ܝܘܡܘܗܝ ܣܓܝܐܐ ܪܗܛ ܗܘܐ ܠܫܘܠܡܐ ܕܝܘܡܘܗܝ.
For the impudent, it would be harmful and reckless to know the date of the end of his life. If he knew it to be long, he would be impious, and if he knew it to be short, he would murmur. It would be a great danger for man to know the number of his days: he would interrupt his many days, he would run to the end of his days.

### 219-230. Ignorance of the last day prepares us for it


[p. 54] {#po.37.p54}
ܝܕܥܬܐ ܟܕ ܬܪܥܐ ܟܣܝܬܢ ܠܝܘܡܢ ܐܚܪܝܐ ܠܐ ܝܕܥ ܐܢܫ ܠܝܘܡ ܡܘܬܗ ܕܒܟܠܙܒܢ ܢܗܘܐ ܚܝܠܬܢ ܐܠܘ ܓܝܪ ܝܕܥ ܗܘܐ ܡܪܐ ܒܝܬܐ ܐܡܬܝ ܐܬܐ ܓܢܒܐ ܠܡܓܢܒܘ
Ignorance, like a veil, has hidden our last day from us. One does not know the day of his death, in order to always be courageous. For if the master of the house knew when the thief would come to steal, he would sleep all day but would be vigilant at that moment.
ܕܡܟ ܗܘܐ ܟܠܗ ܝܘܡܐ ܘܒܗܝ ܫܥܬܐ ܡܬܬܥܝܪ ܗܘܐ
If he knew it, he would become slothful; but since he does not know it, he is courageous.
ܐܠܘ ܓܝܪ ܝܕܥ ܗܘܐ ܡܬܬܚܠܛ ܗܘܐ ܘܗܫܐ ܕܠܐ ܝܕܥ ܚܝܠܬܢ ܗܘ ܡܛܠ ܕܠܐ ܝܕܥ ܠܫܥܬܐ ܒܟܠ ܫܥܐ ܡܬܬܥܝܪ ܗܘܐ
Because he does not know the hour, he makes himself vigilant at every hour.


[p. 55] {#po.37.p55}

### FIFTH MEMRA ON NICOMEDIA
ܡܐܡܪܐ ܚܡܝܫܝܐ ܕܥܠ ܢܝܩܘܡܝܕܝܐ
1-20. Stopping for fear that Anger may stop us.
ܐܬܬܥܝܪܘ ܕܠܐ ܢܬܬܥܝܪ ܥܠܝܢ ܪܘܓܙܐ ܬܐܘ ܢܬܬܥܝܪ ܘܢܒܥܐ ܪ̈ܚܡܐ ܕܠܐ ܢܬܬܥܝܪ ܥܠܝܢ ܪܘܓܙܐ ܡܢܫܠܝܐܝܬ ܐܝܟ ܕܐܬܬܥܝܪ ܥܠ ܣܕܘܡܝܐ ܕܠܐ ܐܬܬܥܝܪܘ ܠܡܒܥܐ ܪ̈ܚܡܐ ܐܬܬܥܝܪ ܩܠܝܠ ܠܘܬ ܝܗܘܒܐ ܕܢܣܓܘܢ ܠܟ ܝܗܒܬܗ ܡܛܠ ܕܥܢܐ ܠܟܠܙܒܢ ܠܕܩܪܐ ܠܗ ܘܝܗܒ ܒܣܓܝܐܘܬܐ ܠܕܡܩܘܐ ܗܢܐ ܬܥܝܪܐ ܐܚ̈ܝ ܠܐ ܬܚܙܘܢܝܗܝ ܐܝܟ ܬܥܝܪܐ
Come! Let us stop, let us implore mercy, for fear that anger may stop us unexpectedly, as it stopped the Sodomites who had not stopped to implore mercy. Stop a little near the Giver, so that his gifts to you may be abundant. For he always answers whoever invokes him and he gives abundantly to him who perseveres. This stop, brothers, do not regard it as a stop, for when a stop is made on earth, God constitutes a treasure for us in the heavens.


[p. 56] {#po.37.p56}
ܡܛܠ ܕܐܡܬܝ ܕܗܘܐ ܬܥܝܪܐ ܒܐܪܥܐ ܣܝܡ ܠܢ ܣܝܡܬܐ ܒܫܡܝܐ ܡܢ ܩܕܝܡ ܡܬܬܥܝܪܝܢ ܗܘܝܢ ܐܡܬܝ ܕܡܬܬܥܝܪܝܢ ܗܘܝܢ ܡܢ ܠܐܘܬܐ ܗܫܐ ܡܥܕܪܝܢܢ ܠܢܦܫܢ ܡܛܠ ܕܥܠܝܢܢ ܠܦܘܠܚܢܐ ܕܨܘܡܐ ܡܨܐ ܐܢܫ ܕܒܚܘܒܐ ܕܨܘܡܐ ܢܣܕܘܩ ܟܬܒܐ ܕܚܘܒܬܗ ܗܢܐ ܬܥܝܪܐ ܙܥܘܪܐ
Formerly we used to stop when we were overwhelmed with fatigue. Now we are helping our soul, for we have set ourselves to the labor of fasting. Someone can, by loving fasting, tear up the schedule of his debt. This brief stop is a rampart against the stop. 21-40. Do not worry about this stop.
ܐܣܛܪܛܝܘܛܐ ܗܘ ܠܘܩܒܠ ܬܥܝܪܐ ܠܐ ܬܬܥܝܩܘܢ ܥܠ ܗܢܐ ܬܥܝܪܐ ܬܐܘ ܢܬܬܥܝܪ ܩܠܝܠ ܕܠܐ ܢܬܬܥܝܪ ܐܝܟ ܗܢܘܢ ܕܐܬܬܥܝܪܘ
Come! Let us stop for an instant, for fear of being stopped, as those were stopped; they, indeed, because they did not stop for an instant, here they are stopped for eternity.


[p. 57] {#po.37.p57}
ܗܢܘܢ ܓܝܪ ܡܛܠ ܕܠܐ ܐܬܬܥܝܪܘ ܩܠܝܠ ܗܐ ܐܬܬܥܝܪܘ ܠܥܠܡ ܥܠܡܝܢ ܠܐ ܢܬܬܥܝܩ ܥܠ ܗܢܐ ܬܥܝܪܐ ܡܛܠ ܕܗܝܕܝܢ ܥܩܬܐ ܦܘܠܚܢܐ ܐܚܪܢܐ ܗܝ ܐܠܐ ܢܬܬܥܝܪ ܡܢ ܬܪ̈ܝܗܘܢ ܡܢ ܦܘܠܚܢܐ ܘܡܢ ܥܩܬܗܘܢ ܐܠܘ ܓܝܪ ܬܥܝܪܐ ܗܘܐ ܦܘܠܚܢܐ
Let us not worry about this stop, for then the worry is a second work. But let us stop from both: from works and from their worries. For if the stop becomes a work and its worry cannot be stopped, our heart is still at work, our spirit is still a laborer.
ܘܥܩܬܗ ܠܐ ܡܨܝܐ ܕܬܬܬܥܝܪ ܠܒܢ ܥܕܟܝܠ ܒܦܘܠܚܢܐ ܗܘ ܪܥܝܢܢ ܥܕܟܝܠ ܦܠܚܐ ܗܘ ܛܒ ܗܘ ܕܦܠܚ ܡܢ ܕܡܬܬܥܝܪ ܘܡܪܛܢ ܠܗ ܓܝܪ ܠܐܘܬܐ ܛܒܐ ܗܝ ܡܢ ܪܛܢܐ ܩܛܘܠܐ ܐܒܕܘ ܓܝܪ ܥܒܪ̈ܝܐ ܒܡܕܒܪܐ ܡܛܠ ܕܪܛܢܘ ܐܝܟ ܕܐܬܬܥܝܪܘ
Better is he who works than he who stops and murmurs. For him, indeed, fatigue is preferable to murderous murmuring. The Hebrews perished in the desert, for they murmured like people who were stopped. Do not murmur, O you who are stopped, for fear that you may be an artisan of sin. 41-70. God compensates for the time one devotes to him.


[p. 58] {#po.37.p58}
ܠܐ ܬܪܛܢ ܐܘ ܕܐܬܬܥܝܪܬ ܕܠܐ ܬܗܘܐ ܥܒܘܕܐ ܕܚܛܝܬܐ ܐܠܗܐ ܡܦܪܥ ܠܙܒܢܐ ܕܡܬܝܗܒ ܠܗ ܚܙܝ ܕܗܢܐ ܬܥܝܪܐ ܡܒܪܟ ܘܡܣܓܐ ܠܟ ܥܠܠܬܟ ܕܚܠܦ ܥܠܠܬܟ ܬܥܝܪܐ ܗܟܢܐ ܛܒ ܗܘ ܐܝܟ ܕܠܐ ܛܒ ܠܥܠܠܬܟ ܚܦܝܛܘܬܐ ܪܥܠܬܐ
Consider that this stop blesses and increases your harvests, that instead of working at your harvests such a stop is good, just as a greedy haste is unfavorable to your harvests. Fast instead of working at your construction, so as to be able to live in it. Why has God stopped your construction? It is because for it you are exhausting yourself.
ܨܘܡ ܚܠܦ ܒܢܝܢܟ ܕܬܫܟܚ ܕܬܥܡܪ ܒܗ ܠܡܢܐ ܐܠܗܐ ܐܬܬܥܝܪ ܒܢܝܢܟ ܕܥܠܘܗܝ ܠܐܐ ܐܢܬ ܐܠܐ ܢܚܙܐ ܡܪܝܐ ܕܐܬܬܥܝܪܬ ܘܗܘ ܡܥܕܪ ܠܦܘܠܚܢܟ ܡܨܐ ܕܢܦܪܘܥ ܠܚܦܝܛܘܬܟ ܡܛܠ ܕܒܚܘܒܗ ܐܬܬܥܝܪܬ
But let the Most High perceive that you have stopped, then he himself comes to the aid of your work. He can compensate for your ardor, since it is out of love for him that it has stopped. He can compensate for your stop, since, for you, work is preferable. Choose the stop that is more useful to you than the exhaustion that is harmful to you.


[p. 59] {#po.37.p59}
£ ܬ ܐܐܬ ܐ ܠܬܬ ܨܬ ܧ ܐܨ ܬܛ ܐ ܠܐܬ ܘܨܬ ܢܨ
One can stop in any situation, in good health as in sickness. When you come to suffer in good health, it is as if you were becoming poor. Perhaps some builders are sad because their constructions have stopped.
,»8% ܬܬ ܐܬܘܬ &ܘܙ ܬܟ ܕܐܕ ܟܬ ܬܘܨ ,ܐܐܐܧ ܬܬ ܬܟ ܣ ܐ ܢܐ ܬܩ ܛ ܨܐ ܗ ܐܬܧܩ ܐܟ ,ܬܬܐܚܨ⁄» ܬ ܛܬܬ ܐܢܙܢ ,ܐܬܐܐܐ ܨܐܐ ܢܐܬ :@
Let them implore for the houses built previously, for fear that they may become tombs. Perhaps some vine-growers are murmuring because harvests and wine-presses have yielded nothing. Let them offer prayers for the previous harvest, for fear that it may perish with those who drink it. Laborious women are anxious in their prayers, for the cloths they were weaving have stopped. Let them pray, for fear of being buried unexpectedly with what they have clothed themselves with. In our souls we are not reassured, because we are in worry for our affairs. 77-94. The recklessness of men.


[p. 60] {#po.37.p60}
ܣܣ ܐܬܐ ܬܘܬܐ ܨܢܐܙ ܢܨ ܐܐܐ ܐ ܐܬ ܘܬܐܕ ܐܐܢܙ ܕܙܘ ܘܙܘ ܢܐ ܕܬ ܐܐܐܧ ܢܬܘ ܐܬܣ?ܩܙ ܩܐ ܐ ܬ ܨ ܐܬܢܐ ܨܐ ܬܝܐ ܨܐܬ ܐܨܦ ܘ ܆ ܐܐܐ ܐ ܛ 4« ܐܢܙ ܙܐ ܕ ܐܬܐܐ ܬܬܗ ܬܐ ܙܐܬ ܬܙ
Behold, anger has devastated cities, and our thoughts are stopping in the mire. Perhaps our condemnation has been pronounced, and behold, our heart is feeding on ashes. The lightning has fallen, it has burned and devastated, and that seems to us like a trifle. Half of the people have disappeared, and we, with the other half, we are reckless for both. Sand has invaded and covered cities, and dust covers us as well as lime.
The mountains have withdrawn from their place, and we, have we withdrawn from our sins? The mountain has descended from its place, and our heart has not descended from its iniquity.


[p. 61] {#po.37.p61}
ܕܬܐ ܩ »ܬ ܐܬ ܠܐ ܠܬܠ ܐܬܬ ܦܘܐ ܢܬܐ ܐܬܐ ܕܐ ܐܨ
The sea has rushed forth and invaded the land, because our generation has rushed into iniquity. The sea has exceeded its limit, because our generation has exceeded its limit. 95-114. Noah and his ark.
£ ܣ ܢܘܚ ܦܠܚ ܗܘܐ ܒܬܝܒܘܬܐ ܘܒܢܝ̈ ܫܪܒܬܗ ܒܚܛܝܬܐ ܗܘ ܡܩܝܡ ܗܘܐ ܬܝܒܘܬܐ <
Noah was working on the ark and the children of his generation on sin. He was consolidating the ark, and they, their stubbornness. He was applying himself to building himself a roof, and the children of his generation, a fortune. He was preparing his refuge, and they, their abyss; for when the cataracts opened, they took vengeance on the iniquities. The waters suffocated the misers who were suffocated under their goods.


[p. 62] {#po.37.p62}
ܘܗܢܘܢ ܩܘܫܝܐ ܕܠܒܗܘܢ ܗܘ ܡܬܩܢ ܗܘܐ ܠܗ ܬܚܝܬ ܓܓܢܐ ܘܒܢܝ̈ ܫܪܒܬܗ ܩܢܝܢܐ ܗܘ ܡܛܝܒ ܗܘܐ ܠܗ ܚܘܣܝܐ ܘܗܢܘܢ ܫܝܘܠܐ ܡܛܠ ܕܟܕ ܐܬܦܬܚܘ ܙܪ̈ܡܐ ܢܩܡܘ ܒܥܘܠ̈ܐ ܘܚܢܩܘ ܡܝ̈ܐ ܠܪ̈ܚܡܝ ܩܢܝܢܐ
The ark opened its doors; Noah crouched in his work. Author of figures, he lived thanks to the wood; the waters suffocated those who loved riches. How many were open-mouthed before the ark! They said, 'Blessed is his ark.' They were strangers to his salvation, for they were strangers to his construction. 115-136. Working on the ark of penitence.


[p. 63] {#po.37.p63}
ܕܚܢܝܩܝܢ ܗܘܘ ܒܩܢܝܢܗܘܢ
Noah built himself, in cedar, a refuge for him and his own.
ܘܦܬܚܬ ܬܝܒܘܬܐ ܬܪ̈ܥܝܗ̇ ܘܐܬܓܢܝ ܒܗ ܦܠܚܗ̇ ܘܐܬܚܝܝ ܒܩܝܣܐ ܨܝܪ̈ܝ ܨܘܪ̈ܬܐ ܘܚܢܩܘ ܡܝ̈ܐ ܠܪ̈ܚܡܝ ܥܘܬܪܐ ܣ ܪ ܟܡܐ ܕܐܬܕܡܪܘ ܒܬܝܒܘܬܐ
As for us, let there be today haircloth and fasting in place of cedar. With them let us make ourselves an ark, with them let us build ourselves cells, for in it our souls are withdrawn from the anger that has rushed into our years. Let us not despise this precept, like the generations of the days of Noah; but let us despise the fasting displayed publicly as they despised the work of the ark.
ܘܐܡܪܘ ܛܘܒܝܗ̇ ܠܬܝܒܘܬܗ ܘܐܬܢܟܪܘ ܠܦܘܪܩܢܗ̇ ܡܛܠ ܕܐܬܢܟܪܘ ܠܒܢܝܢܗ̇
For if we are lazy regarding penance,


[p. 64] {#po.37.p64}
ܢܦܠܘ ܒܗ̇ ܚܢܝܩܝܢ ܘܐܬܚܝܝ ܒܗ̇ ܦܠܚܝܗ̇ ܠܗܘܢ ܗܘܬ ܚܘܣܝܐ ܘܠܕܠܐ ܕܝܠܗ̇ ܚܣܕܐ ܡܛܠ ܕܟܕ ܦܠܚܝܢ ܗܘܘ ܕܝܠܗ̇ ܕܠܐ ܕܝܠܗ̇ ܡܣܠܝܢ ܗܘܘ ܒܗ̇
Penance, for its part, is practiced by a small number who return to it. Thus, those who have practiced it will be saved through it. For its own, it becomes a refuge, but for those who do not belong to it, a reproach. For when its own were practicing it, those who did not belong to it despised it. 137-174. The waters of the flood.
,ܠܐܛ ܘܦܨ ܢܙܐܲܢܘܝܪ̄ ܢܬܐ :ܐܕܨ ܘܬܢܐ ܘܙܬܨܙܙܬܨ ܬ ܐܨ ܐܢܙ ܢܐܗܟ ܐܬܐܬ ܐܬܐ ©; ܐ ܨ« ܢܘܢܐܙ ܕܐܐܐ ܬ ܐܬ ܐܨ ܬܟ :)ܨ ܢ ܙ ܣ
Before the moment (of the flood), death (hovered) over the generation of the days of Noah. Instead of giving themselves to penance, everyone ran to their work. Because they did not run to fasting, the flood ran over them. It surprised everyone at their work, at their work, not at prayer. The waters gushed out from beneath the workers, the waters came out from beneath the plowmen. Torrents rushed around the buildings, over the buildings and the builders.


[p. 65] {#po.37.p65}
ܳܐ / ܙ ܐܬܬܬ ܕܨ ܨ ܬܐܐܐ ܬܬܘ ` ܕ݁ ܬܬܢܘ ܐܐܐ ܙ ܨܐܬ ܬܨ ܐܗܐ:ܙܕܐ ܐ ܕܨܬ ܐ ܘܦܨ ܬ
The floods piled up in the rooms; they struck young bridegrooms in their bridal chambers. Springs gushed out in the porticoes, the sea gushed into the countryside. He who fled from below toward the height, the torrents (rushing down) from the heights met him.
)® '4 ܐܧ 'ܐ 7" ×/ :ܐ 7/ ܐܬܐܬ ܐܬܐ ܟ »ܨ ܨ ܙܡ ܨܐ ّܪܘܙܐ ܙ ܐܘ :ܨ« ܙܐ ܣ ܐܐܐ ܢܐܬ ܙܐܬ ܕܨ ܐܬܐ :ܨ ܙܐ ;ܨ ܨ»«ܐ ܢܬܐ ܕܨܘ ܬܐܐ ܣ & ܠܨ ܐܩܬ ܬܩ ܙܩ ܝ
He who descended from above toward the bottom, the gushing (coming) from below suffocated him. Wherever each one hid, the waters went to meet him. He who entered and hid in a room, the sea roared against him. The waters met the waters, the base of the sky and the abysses. In them was tormented the people who had lived previously as impious.


[p. 66] {#po.37.p66}
ܐ ܐܬܐܬ ܐܬܐܬ ܨ ܐܬ ܟ ܬܐܐ ܨܬ :::@ ܨܐܬ ܐܬ ܬܨ ܬܐܨ |" ×:8 ;ܙܬ :ܟ ܕ ܣܘܣ ܪܘܛ $ܧ& :ܨܐܐ > ܬܐܨ ܐܬܐ ܨ8 ܠ ܨܐܬ ܐܬܒܨ ,ܐܐܟ ܐܪܨܘܐ ܘܨܬ ܐ ܕܨ ܨ ܐܢܐ ܢ ܐܬܬ ܬܐܐ ܐܬ
From all places the waters rushed, for in all places sin had rushed. He liberated the captive seas, so that they might go out (and) seize the free (spaces). The earth where they had lived as impious, he opened springs against it. The heavens under which they provoked him, he made torrents collapse upon them. The ark rose, was esteemed, for, in it, chastity had lodged. 175-198. The destruction of all flesh.


[p. 67] {#po.37.p67}
They began to abandon their goods, and they ran to save their lives. They despised and abandoned their fortunes, in order to save their persons. They threw their treasures to perdition, for, in anger, man prefers his person to his fortune. Now, as they did not flee straight ahead, they began to flee sideways.


[p. 68] {#po.37.p68}
ܐܬܐܬܘ ܛܘܢ ܐܐܐ ܐܢܙ ܕܘܬܣܘܡ ܙ ܐܬܣ ܨ܀ ܨܐܕ & ܐܬܕ ܬܨ ,ܐܐܬ« ܙ ܘܳܐ ܘܢܬ ܢܙ ܐܧ ܛܘ ܙܐ ܬ ܐܐܬ ܐܕܢ ܬܨ« ܨ ܬܐܨ ܨ ܬܐ :ܬܐܨ :ܐܬ ,ܐܨ ܠܙܐܬ ܙܘ :)ܠܨ ܢܬ :ܨܐܬ
As they did not flee from their buildings, the buildings perished with their builders. As they had not stopped for an instant from work, their fatigue increased. 15 began to run toward all the heights, because they had not run toward the unique Height. 15 began to run in all places, because they had not run toward the unique Place.
:ܨܐ ܙܐܘ ܙ ܬܬܐ ¦ ً ܐ ,)ܐܬܙ ܬܘ ܬ ܐܬܐ ܙܐ ܓ ܬܐ ܐܳܬ ܐ ܬ ×« ܕ' ܟ ܐܛ ܕ ܐܐܩ ܘ ܬܨ »ܘܨ ܨ ܕܩܬܰܩܡܢܘ ܛ
As they had not run toward humility, they ran toward the summits of the heights. 15 suffocated on the heights, because they had suffocated in (their) pride. They took refuge on high mountains, because they had not taken refuge in the unique Refuge. They hung themselves from all the trees, because they had not hung themselves from the Unique. 199-236. The destruction of Sodom.


[p. 69] {#po.37.p69}
,]ܬ /ܐ ܬܐܐ ܬ ܐ ¦ :ܘ ܐܬܐܘ ܐܬ ܐܬܪܘ¿ ܢܙܘܨܘܙܢܐ َܐ ܬ ܐ ܬܝܐ .ܚ ܩܕܡ ܙܒܢܐ ܡܘܬܐ ܥܠ ܝܠܕܐ ܕܝܘܡܬܐ ܕܢܘܚ ܚܠܦ ܕܢܪܗܛܘܢ ܠܬܝܒܘܬܐ
And in Sodom, it was the same, when the watchers entered to destroy it. Lot announced the anger, but his sons-in-law were concerned with work. He proclaimed the destruction of the country, but they were concerned with its plantations. He kept repeating the destruction of the country, but they kept repeating the security of the place. As he kept repeating (it) and they answered him, he became their laughingstock. As they began to ridicule (him), claps of thunder and crackling were heard.


[p. 70] {#po.37.p70}
ܟܠܢܫ ܪܗܛ ܠܥܒܕܗ ܡܛܠ ܕܗܢܘܢ ܠܨܘܡܐ ܠܐ ܪܗܛܘ ܛܘܦܢܐ ܥܠܝܗܘܢ ܪܗܛ ܐܕܪܟ ܠܟܠܢܫ ܒܥܒܕܗ ܒܥܒܕܗ ܠܐ ܒܨܠܘܬܐ
Burning coals fell in the guise of dew, and sulfur in the guise of twigs of wood. The fire attacked the young brides, the young brides and their bridal chambers.
ܡܝܐ ܢܦܩܘ ܡܢ ܬܚܝܬ ܥܒܘܕܐ ܡܝܐ ܢܦܩܘ ܡܢ ܬܚܝܬ ܦܠܚܐ ܢܚܠܐ ܪܗܛܘ ܚܕܪ ܒܢܝܢܐ ܥܠ ܒܢܝܢܐ ܘܥܠ ܒܢܝܐ ܓܠܠܐ ܐܬܟܢܫܘ ܒܐܕܪܐ ܡܚܘ ܚܬܢܐ ܒܩܢܛܪܘܢܝܗܘܢ ܡܒܘܥܐ ܢܦܩܘ ܒܐܣܛܘܐ
One was struck by thunder, and the other burned by lightning. At the sound of the explosion, the plunderer was shredded, his mouth was filled with earth. He who had devoured the spoils of the poor, the fire of the Avenger devoured him. He who, thanks to (his) rapine, lived in softness, a sea of fire suffocated him.
ܝܡܐ ܢܦܩ ܒܐܓܘܪܐ ܕܡܢ ܠܬܚܬ ܥܪܩ ܠܥܠܝܐ ܢܚܠܐ ܕܡܢ ܠܥܠܝܐ ܐܪܥܘܗܝ ܕܡܢ ܠܥܠܝܐ ܢܚܬ ܠܬܚܬܝܐ ܢܦܩܐ ܕܡܢ ܠܬܚܬ ܐܚܢܩܬܗ ܟܠܢܫ ܐܝܟܐ ܕܐܬܛܫܝ ܡܝܐ ܐܪܥܘܗܝ ܕܥܠ ܘܐܬܛܫܝ ܒܐܕܪܐ ܝܡܐ ܓܥܐ ܥܠܘܗܝ
All the people saw (it), each one (saw) that it was burning everything. No one extinguished the neighbor's fire, for each was struggling with his own fire. The mother did not have compassion for her daughter to extinguish the fire, for none of them had had pity on the hungry and the destitute. The son blazed before his parents,


[p. 71] {#po.37.p71}
ܡܝܐ ܐܪܥܘ ܡܝܐ
Their combustion took place in the squares, for they let the hungry perish in the squares.
ܫܬܐܣܬܐ ܕܫܡܝܐ ܘܕܬܗܘܡܐ ܒܗܘܢ ܐܬܬܙܝܥ ܥܡܐ ܕܒܚܛܝܬܐ ܚܝ ܡܢ ܩܕܝܡ
They began to run and to seek refuge elsewhere. 237-264. The deliverance of Lot.
ܡܢ ܟܠ ܕܘܟܐ ܪܗܛܘ ܡܝܐ ܡܛܠ ܕܠܟܠ ܕܘܟܐ ܪܗܛ ܚܛܝܬܐ ܫܪܐ ܝܡܡܬܐ ܐܣܝܪܬܐ ܕܬܦܩ ܘܬܬܚܕ ܒܚܐܪܬܐ ܐܪܥܐ ܕܒܗ ܚܝܘ ܒܚܛܝܬܐ ܦܬܚ ܥܠܝܗ ܡܒܘܥܐ ܫܡܝܐ ܕܬܚܝܬܗ ܐܪܓܙܘܗܝ ܐܘܪܕ ܥܠܝܗܘܢ ܢܚܠܐ ܬܝܒܘܬܐ ܐܬܬܪܝܡܬ ܐܬܝܩܪܬ ܡܛܠ ܕܒܗ ܫܟܢܬ ܢܟܦܘܬܐ
Lot was a protection for Sodom; the Sodomites came to strike him down. Justice took him from there for fear that they might be saved because of him. Because they ignored his first deliverance, they perished in the final anger. Thus justice cast its eyes upon him and upon them, with discernment. That he should perish with them, that was not just. That they should live with him, that was not fitting.


[p. 72] {#po.37.p72}
ܚܒܠܐ ܕܟܠ ܒܣܪ ܫܪܝܘ ܕܢܫܒܩܘܢ ܩܢܝܢܝܗܘܢ ܘܪܗܛܝܢ ܗܘܘ ܠܚܝܝܗܘܢ ܒܣܪܘ ܘܫܒܩܘ ܡܡܘܢܝܗܘܢ ܕܢܦܨܘܢ ܢܦܫܬܗܘܢ ܐܪܡܝܘ ܣܝܡܬܗܘܢ ܠܐܒܕܢܐ ܡܛܠ ܕܒܪܘܓܙܐ ܓܒܪܐ ܢܦܫܗ ܥܠ ܡܡܘܢܗ ܐܠܐ ܡܛܠ ܕܠܐ ܥܪܩܘ ܬܪܝܨܐܝܬ
Because he did not commit impiety with them, it did not let him perish with them. And because they did not convert to his call, it did not grant them to be saved with him. Because Lot was not their accomplice in their punishment, he had no part. Because they were strangers to his justice, they were not saved by his prayer. 255-284. We must choose.


[p. 73] {#po.37.p73}
ܫܪܝܘ ܕܢܥܪܩܘܢ ܥܪܩܐ ܥܘܠܐ ܡܛܠ ܕܠܐ ܥܪܩܘ ܡܢ ܒܢܝܢܝܗܘܢ ܐܒܕܘ ܒܢܝܢܐ ܥܡ ܒܢܝܗܘܢ ܡܛܠ ܕܠܐ ܫܠܝܘ ܚܕܐ ܫܥܬܐ ܡܢ ܥܒܕܐ ܐܬܬܘܣܦ ܥܠܝܗܘܢ ܠܐܘܬܐ ܫܪܝܘ ܕܢܪܗܛܘܢ ܠܟܠ ܥܠܝܐ ܡܛܠ ܕܠܐ ܪܗܛܘ ܠܥܠܝܐ ܚܕ ܫܪܝܘ ܕܢܪܗܛܘܢ ܠܟܠ ܕܘܟܐ ܡܛܠ ܕܠܐ ܪܗܛܘ ܠܕܘܟܬܐ ܚܕܐ ܡܛܠ ܕܠܐ ܪܗܛܘ ܠܡܟܝܟܘܬܐ
And we, brothers, let us fear, because we have ignored three deliverances. They ignored only one; we, we have ignored three. And now that the anger has poured out, let us pour out our heart in tears for fear that the final anger may avenge the first deliverances ignored by us. The vintage has passed in fasting, the harvest and the anger together.
ܪܗܛܘ ܠܪܝܫܝ ܪܡܬܐ ܐܬܚܢܩܘ ܒܪܡܬܐ ܡܛܠ ܕܐܬܚܢܩܘ ܒܪܘܡܪܡܐ ܐܬܓܘܣܘ ܒܛܘܪܐ ܪܡܐ ܡܛܠ ܕܠܐ ܐܬܓܘܣܘ ܒܓܘܣܐ ܚܕ ܐܬܬܠܝܘ ܒܟܠ ܐܝܠܢܐ ܡܛܠ ܕܠܐ ܐܬܬܠܝܘ ܒܚܕ ܚܒܠܐ ܕܣܕܘܡ ܘܐܦ ܒܣܕܘܡ ܗܟܢܐ ܗܘܐ ܟܕ ܥܠܘ ܢܛܘܪܐ ܠܡܚܒܠܘܬܗ ܠܘܛ ܡܟܪܙ ܗܘܐ ܠܪܘܓܙܐ ܚܬܢܘܗܝ ܕܝܢ ܚܫܝܒܝܢ ܗܘܘ ܒܥܒܕܐ ܗܘ ܡܟܪܙ ܗܘܐ ܠܚܒܠܐ ܕܐܪܥܐ
Choose like people who can choose; weigh as if on scales, to know which of the (two) pans must dominate, (which) must prevail over its companion. We will hasten toward riches or else we will beg for (our) lives. If there is no discernment in us, well! we will be possessed by the Evil One. The Evil One who deprives of intelligence buys our heart and our intelligence. Everything that a man possesses, he exchanges for his life, it is said.


[p. 74] {#po.37.p74}
ܢܚܢܢ ܒܥܕܢܐ ܕܪܘܓܙܐ ܢܬܠ ܢܦܫܢ ܚܠܦ ܩܢܝܢܢ ܡܚܒܠ ܢܦܫܬܐ ܝܕܥ ܕܢܦܫܐ ܝܬܝܪܐ ܡܢ ܟܠ ܢܚܢܢ ܠܐ ܝܕܥܝܢܢ ܥܠ ܢܦܫܐ ܡܢܐ ܐܝܬܝܗ ܘܡܢܐ ܝܩܪܗ ܐܝܬ ܠܢ ܬܘܒ ܡܪܕܝܐ ܡܘܬܐ ܚܒܪܗ ܕܣܛܢܐ
We, at the hour of anger, give our life instead of our goods. The destroyer of lives knows that life is worth more than everything. We, we do not know, regarding life, what its nature and its price are. There is for us, once again, a pedagogue: death, the friend of Satan. 285-310. One must "stop" to weep and implore.


[p. 75] {#po.37.p75}
ܢܬܬܚܪܬ ܠܡܒܟܐ ܘܠܡܬܟܫܦܘ ܐܢ ܢܗܘܐ ܐܒܠܐ ܒܥܕܥܕܐ ܐܒܠܐ ܦܪܣ ܥܠ ܥܕܥܕܐ ܬܚܦܝܬܐ ܘܐܦܢ ܥܕܥܕܐ ܡܒܘܥܐ ܗܘ ܒܣܝܡܐ ܘܒܘܣܡܐ ܡܪܘܐ ܠܟܠ ܒܪܢܫ ܚܘܒܐ ܒܗ ܛܥܡ ܛܥܡܐ ܕܐܒܠܐ ܡܛܠ ܚܣܘܪܗ ܕܚܒܪܗ ܡܒܛܠ ܠܟܠ ܥܒܕܐ ܡܦܣܩ ܠܟܠ ܒܢܝܢܐ
Let mourning occur in a feast, the mourning spreads a veil over the feast. And although the feast is a sweet spring, and sweetness quenches the thirst of every man, love, in him, has taken on the taste of mourning, because of the disappearance of his friend. It stops all work, it interrupts all construction. It ceases everything that is advantageous, it does what is not advantageous.
ܡܫܬܠܐ ܡܢ ܟܠ ܡܕܡ ܕܝܘܬܪܢܐ ܥܒܕ ܡܕܡ ܕܠܐ ܝܘܬܪܢܐ ܠܬܪ̈ܝܢ ܓܝܪ ܥܠ̈ܠܢ ܫܦܝܪ ܗܘ ܕܢܬܬܚܪܬ ܘܢܬܒܛܠ ܥܠ ܢܦܫܬܢ ܚܕܐ ܡܛܠ ܗܢܘܢ ܕܚܣܪܘ ܘܐܚܪܬܐ ܡܛܠ ܢܦܫܬܢ ܥܠ ܗܢܘܢ ܢܒܟܐ ܒܚܘܒܐ ܘܥܠ ܢܦܫܬܢ ܢܬܟܫܦ ܒܕܚܠܬܐ ܒܚܕܐ ܡܚܘܬܐ ܬܪ̈ܝܢ ܝܘܬܪ̈ܢܝܢ ܢܩܢܐ ܝܘܬܪܢܐ ܠܢܦܫܬܢ ܘܠܐܚܝܢ
For two reasons it is normal to stop and concern ourselves with (our) lives: one, because of those who have disappeared, and the other, because of (our) lives. For the former, let us weep with love, and for (our) lives, let us implore with fear. At one stroke, we will draw a double profit: an advantage for (our) lives and for our brothers. If a dead person stops, for days, his whole family of brothers and relatives,


[p. 76] {#po.37.p76}
ܐܢ ܡܝܬܐ ܚܕ ܡܒܛܠ ܠܝܘܡܬܐ
his sister must stop, in her despondency.
ܠܟܠܗ ܒܝܬ ܐܚܘܗܝ ܘܩܪ̈ܝܒܘܗܝ ܚܬܗ ܬܬܒܛܠ ܒܥܩܬܐ ܥܠܡܐ ܗܢܐ ܒܝܬܗ ܗܘ ܕܐܕܡ
This world is the family of Adam: a great misfortune exists in the family of our father.


[p. 77] {#po.37.p77}
ܐܒܠܐ ܪܒܐ ܐܝܬ ܒܒܝܬ ܐܒܘܢ ܒܢ̈ܝܗ ܘܒܢ̈ܬܗ ܕܐܕܡ ܢܒܟܐ ܡܛܠ ܟܐܒܢ ܚܕܝܕܐ ܐܠܗܐ ܡܠܦ ܠܢ ܒܗܢܘܢ ܕܩܕܡܘܢ ܒܩܕܡܝ̈ܐ ܚܟܡܢܢ ܘܒܡܨܥܝ̈ܐ ܐܬܪܕܝܢܢ ܝܠܦܢܢ ܡܢܐ ܗܘܐ ܠܩܕܡܝ̈ܐ ܘܠܡܨܥܝ̈ܐ ܘܚܙܝܢܢ ܠܐܚܪ̈ܝܐ ܐܢ ܚܝܠܬܢܢ ܐܝܟ ܩܕܡܝ̈ܐ ܢܣܓܐ ܝܬܝܪ ܡܢ ܩܕܡܝ̈ܐ ܘܐܢ ܚܒܝ̈ܠܐܢܢ ܝܬܝܪ ܡܢ ܡܨܥܝ̈ܐ
Sons and daughters of Adam, let us weep because of our common pain. 311-334. God instructs us through those who preceded us. Thanks to the first, we have become wise, and we have been instructed near the middle ones. We have learned what happened to the first and the middle ones, and we have considered the last. If we are courageous like the first, we will multiply more than the first. But if we are lazier than the middle ones, we will be tormented more than the middle ones. And if we are impious like the last, we will be the first for punishment.
ܢܫܬܢܩ ܝܬܝܪ ܡܢ ܡܨܥܝ̈ܐ ܘܐܢ ܪ̈ܫܝܥܐܢܢ ܐܝܟ ܐܚܪ̈ܝܐ ܢܗܘܐ ܩܕܡܝ̈ܐ ܠܬܒܥܬܐ ܘܐܢ ܫܦܝܪ̈ܐܢܢ ܐܝܟ ܐܚܪ̈ܝܐ ܢܗܘܐ ܩܕܡܝ̈ܐ ܠܦܘܪܥܢܐ ܛܘܒܘܗܝ ܠܗܘ ܕܚܒܠ ܠܩܕܡܝ̈ܐ ܕܒܗܘܢ ܢܕܚܠܘܢ ܡܨܥܝ̈ܐ ܬܫܒܘܚܬܐ ܠܗܘ ܕܡܚܐ ܠܡܨܥܝ̈ܐ ܕܒܗܘܢ ܢܬܪܕܘܢ ܐܚܪ̈ܝܐ ܛܘܒܘܗܝ ܠܗܘ ܕܥܡܠ ܥܠ ܩܕܡܝ̈ܐ ܘܠܐ ܬܡܐܢ ܐܦ ܥܠ ܡܨܥܝ̈ܐ ܘܠܐ ܡܢ ܐܚܪ̈ܝܐ ܥܕܠ ܡܛܠ ܕܠܡܥܕܪܘ ܡܣܪܗܒ ܗܘ ܬܫܒܘܚܬܐ ܠܗܘ ܕܚܘܛܪܗ ܪܕܐ ܠܚܣܝܪ̈ܝ ܪܥܝܢܐ ܠܝܘܬܪܢܗܘܢ
And if we are pleasing like the last, we will be the first for reward. Blessed is he who destroyed the first, in order to frighten the middle ones. Praise to him who struck the middle ones, in order to instruct the last. Blessed is he who was active for the first, and also labored for the middle ones. He did not grow weary of the last either, for, in bringing help, he is eager. Praise to him whose staff repressed, in their interest, the reckless. Praise to him whose tenderness has pity, in their interest, on those who convert.


[p. 78] {#po.37.p78}


[p. 79] {#po.37.p79}

### SIXTH MEMRA ON NICOMEDIA
60116 ܬܐܐܐ ܐ ,ܐܝܬ ܗܘܐ ܡܪܝܐ ܐܕ ܐܬܕ /݇܇ ܬܐܨܙܪܐ :ܨ ܨܬ ܐܩܣ ܐ ܢܐܢܬ ܘܨ ©ܧ ܨܡܟ ܨ ܧ )& ܐܬܢܐܬ ܨ /9 ܐܬܬ ܨ ° ܬܐ ܠܬ = ܬ݀ ܐ ܐܨ ܐܬ ܐܐ ܘܙܬ ܘܟ ܢܨ ܢܘܐܐ = ¢ ܐܬܐܐ ܐܬܐܬ ܐ̄ܐܬ ܙܐܘ ܕܐ £ ܬܐ ܬ ܐܐܬܵ ܘ :}
1-10. The rapidity of divine action. There was, Lord, a violent earthquake, because your will gave a sign. It does not take, to give a sign to your work or to delay (it), more than a blink of an eye. Lightning is faster than everything, for from the beginning it has its completion. Faster than it even, your work presses with your command. In a single blink of an eye, your command and your work are linked. 11-44. Examples of this rapidity: the battle against Amalek.


[p. 80] {#po.37.p80}
ܣܣ < ܆ ܛܐ« »ܐܬܐܐ ܨ ܬܐܙܙܐ ܐܬܐܬ« »ܨ ܀ ܐܬ ܛܘ ܬ ܐ ܬܕ ܐ ܨ ܐ ܛ ܐܧ ® ;ܐܙ ܬܨ ܨ ܐܘ ݀ܬ ܙ ܐ ܐܬܐܬ ܨܘ ܐܬܬ ܕܐܨ ܐܬܢܐܬ ܐܨ ܬܐܨ ܩܢ ܐܢܒܨ
Thus testify the battle and the action against Amalek; in it, indeed, both were exercised. Moses (was there) for us as a mirror; he rendered visible this hidden (mystery); for each time his arms were raised, immediately an effect resulted from it. If, through the stutterer with the heavy (tongue) the work rushed to your sign, how much faster must your sign be, through your Beloved (with the) rapid (action). Creation and destruction.
ܨܐܬܐ ܨܐܬ :»® ܕܐܨ ܐܬܙ ܬܐ :ܐ ¥
At the hour when the creatures were formed by your Son (with the) rapid (action),


[p. 81] {#po.37.p81}
When your sign was sketched, your will was immediately accomplished. Thus, at the hour of destruction as at the hour of construction, where your will made a sign, there and then there was ruin. At your sign, light and swift, anger and mercy hasten. Just as your lightness does not tire, your tranquility is not troubled. Your anger and your mercy hastened together toward the same city. One half was overturned by your anger, and one half survived thanks to your mercy. The exodus from Egypt.
ܨܬ ܤܣ '/ ܆ »)ܐܬܐܐ »ܐܨܨ ܬ »ܐܙ ܙܐ ܘ ܙܨܘܕܐ ܠ»܀ ܬܐܘ ݀& :݁ :© ܙ :ܕ ܬ 'ܐܢܕܙ
One and the same night was reserved in Egypt for vengeance and for deliverance. In it perished the firstborn of the Egyptians; in it survived the firstborn of the Hebrews. That day also was reserved for ruin and destruction. The order that destroyed the cities profits others by provoking fear. 45-66. He operates everything with wisdom.


[p. 82] {#po.37.p82}
ܡܤ ܣܣ ܐܐܐ ܐܬܐܬ ܐܠܐܬܐܬܐ))ܐܐ '¿ ¦ ܬܬ ܐ ܐܐܬ ܕ :ܐܐܬ ܙ ܨ ܐ
The bodies that the Creator destroyed, he did not destroy uselessly. He created them with intelligence and destroyed them with wisdom. With wisdom, the Creator operates constructions and destructions. In his act of creation, construction; and in his act of destruction, confusion. If he created with wisdom, with wisdom also he destroyed.


[p. 83] {#po.37.p83}
ܐ ܐܬܬ > ܢܸܣܬܬܨܣܨܳܐ ܬܘܐ &ܩܨܐܩܢܬ ܐܘܩܬ ܐܗܝܐ ܐܨ »ܐܨܘ }« :4« #/ ܐܬܐܬ ܐ¡ ܬܨ ܗܙ ݀ܬ £ ܙܬ & ܐܬܙܘ ܐ ܕܬ ܐܬܐ ܨܧ :ܗܬ ܕܨ ݇ܬ ܬ ّܐ ܐ :)ܐܐ ܬܬ« ܹܐ ,ܬܙ ܧܐ8»« ܐܘ ,ܐܬܬ« ܐܬܐܐܐ ܬܐܐ ܘ ܬ« :ܐ ,ܬܬܐ« :}ܐܤ ܐܐ ܬܐ ܬܐܐ ܙܐ ܕܙ
With the same wisdom as when he created, he destroys when he destroys. If his creation is good, wise also is the destruction. When the Omniscient destroys, his destruction is still a creation. Every creation that is not made with knowledge is a destruction, is a presumption. Everything collapses, for everything holds together thanks to knowledge. Thus, when anger destroys, he gives a proof of his justice.

### 67-76. He operates everything by grace


[p. 84] {#po.37.p84}
ܐܝܟ ܕܒܛܝܒܘܬܗ ܒܪܐ ܐܢܘܢ، ܝܗܒ ܐܦ ܚܘܝܐ ܕܛܝܒܘܬܗ. ܒܛܝܒܘܬܗ ܓܝܪ ܟܢܫ ܐܢܘܢ، ܩܕܡ ܪܘܓܙܐ ܐܘ ܚܛܝܬܐ. ܕܐܝܟ ܫܘܪܝܐ ܗܟܢܐ ܚܪܬܐ، ܕܛܝܒܘܬܗ ܫܪܝܬ ܘܫܠܡܬ. ܒܛܝܒܘܬܗ ܐܥܠ ܐܢܘܢ،
Just as he created them by goodness, he also gave them the proof of his grace. It is indeed by goodness that he gathered them before his anger or their impiety. Such an end is similar to the beginning, for it is his grace that began and that ends. He made them enter by goodness, and he made them exit by love. At the beginning and at the end, grace was at their service. 77-96. Grace and justice cannot be blamed.
ܒܚܘܒܗ ܐܦܩ ܐܢܘܢ. ܒܫܘܪܝܐ ܘܒܚܪܬܐ، ܛܝܒܘܬܐ ܫܡܫܬ ܐܢܘܢ. ܛܝܒܘܬܐ ܘܙܕܝܩܘܬܐ ܠܐ ܡܬܬܟܠܡܢ. ܛܝܒܘܬܟ ܘܙܕܝܩܘܬܟ، ܢ ܠܐ ܡܬܬܟܠܡܢ ܚܕܐ ܡܢ ܚܕܐ،
Your grace and your justice are blameless, neither one nor the other, for both are above the blames of our freedom. Your grace did not model forms for a wage nor for a price. Your justice destroyed them neither unjustly nor for gifts. For gratuitous is your donation; whoever receives it cannot blame.


[p. 85] {#po.37.p85}
ܕܬܪ̈ܬܝܗܝܢ ܠܥܠ ܐܢܝܢ ܡܢ ܬܟܠܬܐ ܕܚܐܪܘܬܢ. ܛܝܒܘܬܟ ܠܐ ܨܪܬ ܨܘܪ̈ܬܐ، ܡ ܠܐ ܒܐܓܪܐ ܘܠܐ ܒܛܝܡܐ. ܙܕܝܩܘܬܟ ܚܒܠܬ ܐܢܝܢ، ܠܐ ܒܥܘܠܐ ܘܠܐ ܒܫܘܚܕܐ.
For deserved is your correction; it cannot be harmful to us. For it is not futile and disordered; there is no one who can murmur. For you do not strike blindly; there is no one who can blaspheme. Who turns away from the Giver when he does him good? Who complains of the Judge when he strikes him justly?

### 97-112. Sweetness and severity of grace and justice


[p. 86] {#po.37.p86}
ܕܡܓܢ ܗܝ ܡܘܗܒܬܟ، ܠܐ ܡܬܬܟܠܡ ܢܣܒܗ. ܕܡܚܝܒ ܗܝ ܡܚܘܬܟ، ܠܐ ܡܬܚܒܠ ܚܒܘܠܗ. ܕܠܐ ܗܝ ܣܪܝܩܐ ܘܕܠܐ ܣܕܪܐ، ܠܝܬ ܕܡܬܪܛܢ ܒܗ. ܕܠܐ ܗܝ ܥܘܝܪܐ ܡܚܘܬܟ، ܠܝܬ ܕܡܓܕܦ ܒܗ. ܡܢܘ ܪܚܩ ܡܢ ܝܗܘܒܐ، ܣ ܐ ܟܕ ܥܒܕ ܠܗ ܛܒܬܐ. ܡܢܘ ܡܬܪܥܐ ܥܠ ܕܝܢܐ، ܟܕ ܡܚܐ ܠܗ ܒܙܕܝܩܘܬܐ. ܒܣܝܡܘܬܐ ܘܩܫܝܘܬܐ ܕܛܝܒܘܬܐ ܘܕܙܕܝܩܘܬܐ.
How sweet is your grace; it loves whoever is worthy of it. In it, indeed, there is discernment; in it, there is also severity. Wise also is your justice, to destroy and to caress. Wisdom is hidden within it, and sweetness is also mingled with it. Your sweetness is severe, for its sweetness is discernment. Your severity is tender, for its rigor is wisdom. While your sweetness is severe, how tender is your rigor! For your grace and your justice mingle with one another; there is therefore no longer any distinction. 113-148. Justice and grace are inseparable.
ܟܡܐ ܒܣܝܡܐ ܛܝܒܘܬܟ، ܕܪܚܡܐ ܠܕܫܘܐ ܠܗ. ܐ ܐܝܬ ܒܗ ܕܝܢܐ ܘܐܝܬ ܒܗ ܩܫܝܘܬܐ. ܚܟܝܡܐ ܐܦ ܙܕܝܩܘܬܟ،
Whoever strips off one title and puts on another is divided between two names. Justice, however, is not divided regarding its name, for its nature does not divide. It has acquired parts by reason of denominations, for it has been called by two names.


[p. 87] {#po.37.p87}
ܠܡܚܒܠܘ ܘܠܡܠܘܫܘ. ܚܟܡܬܐ ܒܗ ܛܫܝܐ، ܘܒܣܝܡܘܬܐ ܐܦ ܒܗ ܡܡܙܓܐ. ܒܣܝܡܘܬܟ ܩܫܝܐ، ܢ
Your knowledge itself, moreover, has varied names and appellations so that, through them, our humanity might be capable of learning your anger and your goodness. Your grace possesses two things: goodness and choice. For when your gifts are made, they are made according to a gratuitous choice.
ܕܒܣܝܡܘܬܗ ܕܝܢܐ ܗܝ. ܩܫܝܘܬܟ ܒܣܝܡܐ، ܕܩܫܝܘܬܗ ܚܟܡܬܐ ܗܝ. ܟܕ ܩܫܝܐ ܒܣܝܡܘܬܟ، ܟܡܐ ܒܣܝܡܐ ܩܫܝܘܬܟ. ܕܛܝܒܘܬܟ ܘܙܕܝܩܘܬܟ ܡܡܙܓܢ ܚܕܐ ܒܚܕܐ،
It is not in pure waste, indifferently, that your grace spreads its treasure. If your gift is despised, it regrets its choice. If justice, with it, is mistreated, it demands reparation for this bad treatment. As they are above sufferings, they are also above prejudices.


[p. 88] {#po.37.p88}
ܘܠܝܬ ܠܗܝܢ ܦܘܪܫܢܐ. ܙܕܝܩܘܬܐ ܘܛܝܒܘܬܐ ܠܐ ܡܬܦܪ̈ܫܢ. ܘܐܝܟ ܕܒܛܝܒܘܬܐ ܒܪܐ ܐܢܘܢ، ܐܦ ܝܗܒ ܠܗܘܢ ܦܘܪܫܢܐ ܕܛܝܒܘܬܗ. ܒܛܝܒܘܬܐ ܓܝܪ ܟܢܫ ܐܢܘܢ ܩܕܡ ܪܘܓܙܗ ܐܘ ܚܛܝܬܐ ܕܝܠܗܘܢ. ܕܡܝܐ ܓܝܪ ܚܪܬܐ ܗܕܐ ܠܪܝܫܝܬܐ، ܕܛܝܒܘܬܗ ܓܝܪ ܫܪܝܬ ܘܫܠܡܬ. ܒܛܝܒܘܬܐ ܐܥܠ ܐܢܘܢ، ܘܒܚܘܒܐ ܐܦܩ ܐܢܘܢ. ܒܪܝܫܝܬܐ ܘܒܚܪܬܐ، ܛܝܒܘܬܐ ܫܡܫܬ ܠܗܘܢ. ܛܝܒܘܬܟ ܘܙܕܝܩܘܬܟ
If humanity, with it, is mistreated, then both come to its aid. There, indeed, where there is deprivation, your grace opens its treasures. And there where there is refusal, your justice shows its rod. But just as it is an affliction if your grace gives nothing, and it is an intolerable deprivation for us if it closes its refuge; likewise also, it is a violation of the law if your justice does not correct, and it is an extreme damage for us if the rod spares us. 149-160. Grace and justice do not fail.


[p. 89] {#po.37.p89}
ܠܐ ܡܬܬܟܣܢ ܚܕܐ ܡܢ ܚܕܐ، ܕܬܪ̈ܬܝܗܝܢ ܓܝܪ ܠܥܠ ܐܢܝܢ ܡܢ ܬܟܣܝܬܐ ܕܚܐܪܘܬܢ.
But his grace does not withhold its gifts far from humanity,


[p. 90] {#po.37.p90}
ܕܗܐ ܫܡܫܗ ܠܒܪ̈ܝܬܗ ܣ ܕܠܐ ܨܐܕܐ ܢܐܕܐ ܐܬܬܘ ܀ ܐܨ ܩܘܦ ܙܐ ܐܠܨܬ ܨܐܕܐ ܬܬܘ ܘܨܐ ܐ ܓ ܐ ܕܨ ܬܬܘ ܬܐܬܬܘ ܐܬܬ ܕܐܬܬܬ ܐܙ«
for behold, his sun for his creatures rises on the good and the wicked. Justice also does not interrupt its aid to humanity, for behold, there are fears in every place, and terrors in every spot. At the hour when the city fell, grace did not save it. It knew that it was for utility that justice, there, had reprimanded. 161-172. Recourse without delay to the remedies of grace.


[p. 91] {#po.37.p91}
ܐܬܘܣܐ ܨܐܕܐ ܬܬܘ ܐܬܬ ܦܘܨ ܠܬܬܦܐ :® /4 ܐܬܬܬ ܟܐ ܐ »ܬ« ܐ ܕܬܬܬ ܐܨܘ »ܨ } ܐܧ ܨܘ ܬܘܨܘ ܕ ܘܕܐܘܐ '}»« ܩܬܐ ܬܟ ܘܬ ܙܐ ܪ ܐܐܐ ܐܬܬܬ ;ܐ
Do not wound yourselves, but have recourse to the remedies of grace, since it heals, for it is not pleasing to grace to deploy its strength in your sufferings. A bad physician is he who waits for sufferings to increase in order to draw praise from them. Although it heals gratuitously, grace is very unhappy. Grace suffers much to deploy its strength by means of sufferings. There are for it other occasions where it accepts praise. 173-200. At the beginning and at the end, grace.
ܟ ܟ »ܬ‫ ܙ ' ܐܐܟ ܘܢ ܐ ,)ܬܐܐܐ ܐ« ܨܐܐܐ ܐܬܘܬ ܬܨ ܙ ܬܐܨ ܐ ܬܐܐ ܨܘ ܨ 0¡ ܟܙ ܐܐܘ ܵܬ ܐܨ ܐܐ ܐ¡“ ܬ ܐ ܢ
From the beginning, it deployed its strength, and at the end it is praised. Assuredly, it is very possible for it to deploy its strength at both extremities. It created them at the beginning, without their labors, in a totally gratuitous way. They receive at the end since they lent themselves, but they receive much more, gratuitously. Justice ensures retribution; according to the measure, it gives remuneration.


[p. 92] {#po.37.p92}
ܬ ܐܐܬ "ܐܬܙ ܚ ,ܬܨ ܕ & ܛܢܦܘܬ ܐܨ ܘܨ ܐܩܢܐ
Grace also gives in its turn, even more than the measure, not according to the labors of the just,
ܪܐܬ ܦܘ ܐܬܬܬ ܐܢܬܐ ܐܬ ܨ ܐܬܬܬܬ ‫ ܐܬܬܬܬ ܐܬ ܐܬܬ ܛ
Just as someone receives a wage by reason of some labors, he receives infinite and indescribable gifts,
ܬ ܐ ܐ ܘܨܘ ܬܬܘ ܐܬ ܐܬܘܨ ܬܐ ܐܬܢܐܬ ܬܠܬ
His work allowed him to enter, to receive much. It is thanks to these labors that grace loves


[p. 93] {#po.37.p93}
ܐܐ ܧ6 ܐܕܐ ܕܬܪܐ ;ܐܬܘ :ܟ ܬ¿ ܕܬܬ ' ܬܐ ܐܟ ܣܦ ܙܐܬ ܙܐܬ ܨܬ ܐܬ ܐܬܙ ܠ ܬܗ »ܬܠ ܬ ܘܵܙ ,ܐܬܐ :ܐܐܬ ܬܬܨܨ× ܐ ܙܐܬ :ܗ0110
to signal its strength toward those below. Just indeed as to those above it gave, without their labors, in a totally gratuitous way, likewise also to those below it will give, for little, the kingdom. Behold its strength at the beginning, and at the end its praise.

### SEVENTH (MEMRA) ON NICOMEDIA
ܡܕܝܢܬܐ ܕܐܬܚܪܒܬ ܗܘܬ
1-40. Mourning for the destroyed city.


[p. 94] {#po.37.p94}
ܥܠ ܐܪܥܐ ܡܥܝܢܐ ܕܐܒܠܐ ܚܕܬܐ ܡܪܝܪܬܐ ܫܡܥܬܐ ܡܠܝܬ ܡܪܝܪܘܬܐ ܠܐܕܢܐ ܕܐܬܚܒܠ ܫܡܥܗܝܢ ܡܢ ܫܡܥܬܐ ܕܗܟܢܐ ܡܪܝܪܐ ܟܡܐ ܝܬܝܪܐܝܬ ܗܘܬ ܡܪܝܪܘܬܐ ܐܠܘ ܒܩܪܒܐ ܗܘܐ ܚܘܒܠܐ ܕܐܦ ܡܢ ܫܡܥܗ ܚܒܠ ܠܪܚܝܩܐ ܐܝܟܢܐ ܘܒܟܡܐ ܚܒܠ ܠܩܪܝܒܐ ܚܕ ܐܚܐ ܡܢ ܥܣܪܐ ܐܚܝܢ ܕܡܐܬ ܡܟܐ ܠܡܥܩܘ ܠܐܒܗܘܗܝ ܘܚܘܒܠܗ ܕܚܕ ܩܢܝܢܐ ܡܥܡܝ ܠܗ ܠܡܪܐ ܩܢܝܢܐ
The city that was destroyed has become a source of mourning for the earth. The news, bitter news, fills with bitterness the ears of those whose hearing was wounded by such sad news. How much worse the bitterness would have been, in truth, if the disaster had occurred nearby, it which could cause torment, just by its announcement, to those who are far away! How and with how many torments did it actually afflict those who were present! That a single brother should die among ten brothers, that suffices to sadden his father and mother; and the loss of a single possession darkens the one who possesses a fortune.


[p. 95] {#po.37.p95}
ܟܡܐ ܝܬܝܪܐܝܬ ܡܪܝܪ ܐܒܠܐ ܕܟܠ ܡܕܡ ܐܬܚܒܠ ܒܗ ܘܠܐ ܐܫܬܚܪ ܕܢܒܝܐ ܘܠܐ ܐܫܬܚܪ ܕܢܒܝܐ ܘܠܐ ܐܫܬܚܪ ܕܢܒܝܐ ܘܠܐ ܐܫܬܚܪ ܕܢܒܝܐ ܘܠܐ ܐܫܬܚܪ ܕܢܒܝܐ ܘܠܐ ܐܫܬܚܪ ܕܢܒܝܐ ܘܠܐ ܐܫܬܚܪ ܕܢܒܝܐ ܘܠܐ ܐܫܬܚܪ ܕܢܒܝܐ ܘܠܐ ܐܫܬܚܪ ܕܢܒܝܐ ܘܠܐ ܐܫܬܚܪ ܕܢܒܝܐ ܘܠܐ ܐܫܬܚܪ ܕܢܒܝܐ ܘܠܐ ܐܫܬܚܪ ܕܢܒܝܐ ܘܠܐ ܐܫܬܚܪ ܕܢܒܝܐ
How much more bitter is the mourning where everything has been lost, where no one remains to encourage and no descendant to console! Where everything has been lost, consolations no longer count. Where everything has been ruined, nothing can console. Confused there are the damages, confused are the news. Because the narration is confused, the lamentation is also confused. How bitter is this mourning, from the fact that it is cruel!


[p. 96] {#po.37.p96}
ܘܠܐ ܐܫܬܚܪ ܕܢܒܝܐ ܘܠܐ ܐܫܬܚܪ ܕܢܒܝܐ ܘܠܐ ܐܫܬܚܪ ܕܢܒܝܐ ܘܠܐ ܐܫܬܚܪ ܕܢܒܝܐ ܘܠܐ ܐܫܬܚܪ ܕܢܒܝܐ ܘܠܐ ܐܫܬܚܪ ܕܢܒܝܐ ܘܠܐ ܐܫܬܚܪ ܕܢܒܝܐ ܘܠܐ ܐܫܬܚܪ ܕܢܒܝܐ ܘܠܐ ܐܫܬܚܪ ܕܢܒܝܐ ܕܡܥܐ ܕܐܬܬܙܝܥܘ ܩܕܡ ܝܘܡܐ
It is so hard that the mouth cannot express the destructions that occurred there. What sick person, coming out of sickness, increased his joy in this disaster? Because he reached the threshold of death, in his anger, a "woe!" forced his lips. The joyful man did not enjoy his joy, nor the one who recovered his health. The "woe!" became general, for death became general. 41-70. The tears, the cries, and the deaths were prophetic.
ܕܡܥܐ ܢܒܝܘܬܐ ܐܝܬܝܗܘܢ ܗܘܘ ܟܕ ܒܟܝܢ ܗܘܘ ܥܠ ܡܝܬܐ ܥܠ ܢܦܫܗܘܢ ܒܟܝܢ ܗܘܘ ܒܒܟܝܐ ܢܦܫܗܘܢ ܥܠ ܢܦܫܗܘܢ ܕܠܐ ܝܕܥܝܢ ܩܕܡܘ ܘܒܟܘ ܡܚܝܠ̈ܐ ܦܘܡܝܗܘܢ ܥܠ ܢܦܫܗܘܢ ܡܪܡܪܝܢ ܩܠܝܗܘܢ ܥܠܝܗܘܢ ܒܟܝܢ ܒܙܕܩܐ ܕܝܢ ܗܘܐ ܒܟܝܐ ܐܠܐ ܐܝܟ ܐܬܐ ܟܣܝܬܐ ܐܝܟ ܕܒܐܬܐ ܒܚܝܝܗܘܢ ܩܕܡܘ ܘܒܟܘ ܥܠ ܡܘܬܗܘܢ ܟܕ ܒܟܝܢ ܗܘܘ ܥܠ ܡܝܬܝܗܘܢ ܡܘܬܗܘܢ ܐܣܪܗ ܠܡܐܬܐ
The tears shed before this day were prophetic tears. When they wept over the dead, they wept over themselves while weeping. They themselves over themselves, without knowing it, wept in advance, the wretches! Their mouths, over themselves, lamented; their voices wept for them. It is with good reason, however, that the tears occurred, but as a hidden symbol. Indeed, as in a symbol during their life, they wept in advance for their death. While they wept for their dead, their own death hastened its coming.


[p. 97] {#po.37.p97}
ܥܡ ܩܠܐ ܕܐܒܠܐ ܥܠ ܡܝܬܐ ܐܬܬ ܚܪܒܬܐ ܕܚܝܐ ܩܠܐ ܕܐܒܠܐ ܥܠ ܡܝܬܝܗܘܢ ܫܡܥ ܡܘܬܗܘܢ ܢܒܝܐ ܗܘܐ ܩܠܝܠ ܩܕܡ ܙܘܥܐ ܕܗܘܐ ܩܠܝܠ ܒܬܪܗ ܕܠܡܐ ܐܦ ܒܗ ܒܝܘܡܐ ܣܓܝܘ ܡܝܬܐ ܡܢ ܝܘܡܬܐ ܕܩܕܡܘ ܐܬܪܫܡܘ ܡܬܠܐ
With the cry of pain over the dead, came the ruin of the living. The cry of pain over their dead celebrated their own death. It was a prophet, shortly before the tremors that occurred shortly after. Perhaps even on that day the dead were more numerous than on other days, for in advance parables were engraved through these visible events,


[p. 98] {#po.37.p98}
ܒܝܕ ܗܠܝܢ ܕܡܬܚܙܝܢ ܕܒܣܘܓܐܐ ܕܡܝܬܝܗܘܢ ܬܬܪܫܡ ܐܬܐ ܕܡܘܬܗܘܢ ܘܒܣܘܓܐܐ ܕܩܠܝ ܐܒܠܐ ܬܒܟܐ ܡܕܝܢܬܐ ܥܠ ܢܦܫܗ ܕܐܬܦܪܫܬ ܡܢ ܚܒܪ̈ܬܗ ܕܫܦܝܪܐ ܗܘܬ ܡܢ ܚܒܪ̈ܬܗ
so that by the increase of their dead the symbol of their death might be represented, and that in the increase of cries of pain, the city might weep for itself; for it has been separated from its companions, it which was more beautiful than its companions. 71-88. One must mourn.


[p. 99] {#po.37.p99}
ܨ ܐܐ ܡܕܺܝܢܬܳܐ ܕܶܐܬܬܚܰܪܒܰܬ ܐܶܬܰܬ ܬܰܚܪܶܬܳܐ ܠܰܐܪܥܳܐ. ܘܡܶܠܬܳܐ ܚܕܰܬܳܐ ܡܰܪܺܝܪܬܳܐ ܡܰܠܝܰܬ ܡܰܪܺܝܪܽܘܬܳܐ ܠܶܐܕܢܰܝ̈ܗܽܘܢ ܕܰܐܝܠܶܝܢ ܕܶܐܬܬܚܰܫܰܬ ܫܶܡܥܬܗܽܘܢ ܡܶܢ ܗܳܕܶܐ ܡܶܠܬܳܐ ܕܰܟܡܳܐ ܡܰܪܺܝܪܳܐ. ܟܡܳܐ ܝܰܬܺܝܪܳܐܝܺܬ ܗܳܟܺܝܠ ܡܰܪܺܝܪܽܘܬܳܐ ܐܶܠܽܘ ܒܰܩܪܺܝܒܽܘܬܳܐ ܗܘܳܐ ܚܽܘܪܒܳܐ ܕܰܐܦܶܢ ܒܰܡܶܠܬܶܗ ܐܰܥܺܝܩ ܠܰܪ̈ܳܚܽܘܩܶܐ ܟܡܳܐ ܝܰܬܺܝܪܳܐܝܺܬ ܐܰܥܺܝܩ ܠܰܩܪ̈ܺܝܒܶܐ. ܐܶܢ ܚܰܕ ܐܰܚܳܐ ܡܶܢ ܥܶܣܪܳܐ ܐܰܚܺܝ̈ܢ ܡܳܐܶܬ ܣܳܦܶܩ ܠܰܡܥܳܩܽܘ ܠܰܐܒܳܗܰܘ̈ܗܝ ܘܶܐܡܳܗܳܬܶܗ ܘܰܐܒܕܳܢܳܐ ܕܚܰܕ ܩܶܢܝܳܢܳܐ ܡܰܟܡܰܪ ܠܰܐܝܢܳܐ ܕܺܐܝܬ ܠܶܗ ܩܶܢܝܳܢܳܐ. ܟܡܳܐ ܝܰܬܺܝܪܳܐܝܺܬ ܡܰܪܺܝܪܳܐ ܬܰܚܪܶܬܳܐ ܐܰܝܟܳܐ ܕܟܽܠܡܶܕܶܡ ܐܶܬܰܐܒܰܕ ܘܠܳܐ ܐܶܫܬܰܚܰܪ ܕܰܡܒܰܝܰܐ ܘܠܳܐ ܚܰܕ ܡܶܢ ܒܢܰܝ̈ܢܳܫܳܐ.
If we see a corpse surrounded by respect and honored with crowns, sadness invades us, while no one calls for sadness. A dying person who departs saddens us, for our lineage is bound in Adam. Our eye is afflicted before a deceased person, for one is our race come out of Eve. We are saddened over the one who was suffocated, for one is the origin of us all. A hidden love circulates in us, for a kinship exists between us. If for this light mourning our nature is saddened, pained, how much more will our eyes fill with tears because of the city filled with corpses! Who would fill his mouth with laughter when the city is filled with the suffocated? 89-112. The eve and the morning of the disaster.
ܐܰܝܟܳܐ ܕܟܽܠܡܶܕܶܡ ܐܶܬܰܐܒܰܕ ܠܳܐ ܡܶܬܡܰܢܝܳܢ ܒܽܘܝܳܐܶܐ. ܐܰܝܟܳܐ ܕܟܽܠܡܶܕܶܡ ܐܶܬܚܰܪܰܒ ܠܳܐ ܡܶܫܟܰܚ ܕܰܢܒܰܝܰܐ ܡܶܕܶܡ. ܡܚܰܠܛܺܝܢ ܒܶܗ ܚܽܘܪ̈ܒܶܐ ܡܚܰܠܛܺܝܢ ܒܶܗ ܚܰܕ̈ܬܳܬܶܐ. ܡܶܛܽܠ ܕܡܚܰܠܛܳܐ ܡܶܠܬܳܐ ܡܚܰܠܛܳܐ ܐܳܦ ܝܰܠܠܬܳܐ. ܟܡܳܐ ܡܰܪܺܝܪܳܐ ܗܳܢܳܐ ܬܰܚܪܶܬܳܐ ܡܶܛܽܠ ܕܰܩܫܶܐ ܗܽܘ.
15 fell asleep in the evening, and the roofs fell on their bodies. They fell asleep at dawn, and behold their houses collapsed on their bodies. That night they were perhaps more vigilant than on any other night, so that the interruption of their sleep might figure the great stopping of their journey. When at dawn the city awoke, to its affairs everyone returned: the idle women, to their finery,


[p. 100] {#po.37.p100}
ܕܰܟܡܳܐ ܩܫܶܐ ܗܽܘ ܕܠܳܐ ܡܶܫܟܚܳܐ ܦܽܘܡܳܐ ܕܢܶܡܠܰܠ ܚܽܘܪ̈ܒܶܐ ܕܰܗܘܰܘ ܒܶܗ. ܐܰܝܢܳܐ ܟܪܺܝܗܳܐ ܕܰܢܦܰܩ ܡܶܢ ܟܽܘܪܗܳܢܳܐ ܐܰܘܣܶܦ ܚܰܕܽܘܬܶܗ ܒܗܳܢܳܐ ܚܽܘܪܒܳܐ. ܡܶܛܽܠ ܕܰܠܬܰܪܥܳܐ ܕܡܰܘܬܳܐ ܡܛܳܐ ܒܪܽܘܓܙܶܗ ܐܰܠܰܨ ܦܽܘܡܶܗ ܘܶܐܡܰܪ ܘܳܝ. ܠܳܐ ܚܕܺܝ ܚܰܕܽܘܬܶܗ ܓܰܒܪܳܐ ܚܰܕܽܘܬܳܢܳܐ ܘܠܳܐ ܐܰܝܢܳܐ ܕܶܐܬܚܰܠܰܡ. ܘܳܝ ܗܳܟܺܝܠ ܗܘܳܐ ܟܽܠܳܢܳܝܳܐ ܡܶܛܽܠ ܕܡܰܘܬܳܐ ܗܘܳܐ ܟܽܠܳܢܳܝܳܐ. ܕܶܡ̈ܥܶܐ ܕܶܐܬܶܐܫܰܕ̈ܝ ܩܕܳܡ ܗܳܢܳܐ ܝܰܘܡܳܐ ܕܶܡ̈ܥܶܐ ܢܒܺܝܳܬܳܐ ܐܺܝܬܰܝ̈ܗܶܝܢ. ܟܰܕ ܒܳܟܶܝܢ ܗ̄ܘܰܘ ܥܰܠ ܡܺܝ̈ܬܶܐ
the industrious, to their looms. Everyone left for their occupations, the angel of anger, for destruction. They did not know, the wretches, that their death was standing at the doors. The builders left for their constructions. The artisans disposed themselves to work. The judges entered the tribunal. The visitors went out for their visits. At the stroke of three o'clock, at the hour of the sweet light, the sun set for the city, and it found itself deprived of light. 113-124. The sick and their physicians.
ܥܰܠ ܢܰܦܫܳܬܗܽܘܢ ܒܳܟܶܝܢ ܗ̄ܘܰܘ ܒܰܒܟܳܝܳܐ. ܢܰܦܫܳܬܗܽܘܢ ܥܰܠ ܢܰܦܫܳܬܗܽܘܢ ܕܠܳܐ ܝܳܕܥܺܝܢ ܩܕܶܡ ܒܟܰܘ ܗܳܢܽܘܢ ܕܰܠܝ̈ܳܠܶܐ. ܦܽܘܡܰܝ̈ܗܽܘܢ ܥܰܠ ܢܰܦܫܳܬܗܽܘܢ ܡܝܰܠܠܺܝܢ ܩܳܠܰܝ̈ܗܽܘܢ ܥܰܠ ܢܰܦܫܳܬܗܽܘܢ ܒܳܟܶܝܢ. ܒܰܙܕܺܝܩܽܘܬܳܐ ܕܶܝܢ ܗܘܳܐ ܒܟܳܝܳܐ ܐܶܠܳܐ ܐܰܝܟ ܪܳܙܳܐ ܟܰܣܝܳܐ. ܐܰܝܟ ܪܳܙܳܐ ܓܶܝܪ ܒܚܰܝܰܝ̈ܗܽܘܢ ܩܕܶܡ ܒܟܰܘ ܠܡܰܘܬܳܐ ܕܺܝܠܗܽܘܢ. ܟܰܕ ܒܳܟܶܝܢ ܗ̄ܘܰܘ ܥܰܠ ܡܺܝ̈ܬܰܝܗܽܘܢ ܡܰܘܬܳܐ ܕܺܝܠܗܽܘܢ ܐܰܣܪܰܥ ܠܡܶܐܬܳܐ. ܥܰܡ ܩܳܠܳܐ ܕܰܐܢܚܳܬܳܐ ܥܰܠ ܡܺܝ̈ܬܶܐ ܐܶܬܳܐ ܚܽܘܪܒܳܢܳܐ ܕܚܰܝ̈ܶܐ.
The sick suffocated in their beds, they who hoped to rise. The roofs collapsed and buried the sick and the visitors. The sick man there was suffocated, and around him, his visitors. It was at the hour of visiting that anger visited the city. At the hour when the doctors went out for their rounds, at that same hour the watchers went out with orders to destroy 125-150. Funeral processions and gravediggers.


[p. 101] {#po.37.p101}
ܩܳܠܳܐ ܕܰܐܢܚܳܬܳܐ ܥܰܠ ܡܺܝ̈ܬܰܝܗܽܘܢ ܫܰܡܠܺܝ ܠܡܰܘܬܳܐ ܕܺܝܠܗܽܘܢ. ܢܒܺܝܳܐ ܗܘܳܐ ܩܰܠܺܝܠ ܩܕܳܡ ܙܰܘ̈ܥܶܐ ܕܰܗܘܰܘ ܩܰܠܺܝܠ ܒܳܬܰܪ ܟܶܢ. ܕܰܠܡܳܐ ܐܳܦܶܢ ܒܗܰܘ ܝܰܘܡܳܐ ܝܰܬܺܝܪܺܝܢ ܗ̄ܘܰܘ ܡܺܝ̈ܬܶܐ ܡܶܢ ܟܽܠ ܝܰܘ̈ܡܺܝܢ ܡܶܛܽܠ ܕܩܕܶܡ ܐܶܬܪܰܫܰܡܘ ܡܰܬܠܶܐ
The living formed a procession for the dead; the final moment formed a procession for all. They formed a procession for themselves with the dead, for with them they were buried in the squares. Those who formed a procession for the departed, he took with him to the underworld.


[p. 102] {#po.37.p102}


[p. 103] {#po.37.p103}
ܒܰܐܝܠܶܝܢ ܕܡܶܬܚܰܙܶܝܢ ܕܰܒܣܽܘܓܳܐܐ ܕܡܺܝ̈ܬܰܝܗܽܘܢ ܢܶܬܪܰܫܰܡ ܪܳܙܳܐ ܕܡܰܘܬܳܐ ܕܺܝܠܗܽܘܢ.
People went out to form a procession, and they took the path of eternity. The living formed a procession for their living; unexpectedly (they found themselves) among the dead.
ܘܰܒܣܽܘܓܳܐܐ ܕܩܳܠܳܐ ܕܰܐܢܚܳܬܳܐ ܬܶܒܟܶܐ ܡܕܺܝܢܬܳܐ ܠܢܰܦܫܳܗ̇. ܡܶܛܽܠ ܕܶܐܬܦܰܪܫܰܬ ܡܶܢ ܚܰܒܪ̈ܳܬܳܗ̇ ܗܳܝ ܕܫܰܦܺܝܪܳܐ ܗ̄ܘܳܬ ܡܶܢ ܚܰܒܪ̈ܳܬܳܗ̇. ܐܶܢ ܚܳܙܶܝܢܰܢ ܦܰܓܪܳܐ ܚܰܕ ܕܰܡܝܰܩܰܪ ܘܰܡܟܰܠܰܠ ܒܟܶܠܺܝܠܶܐ ܬܐܐ ܐ ܬ »0 & ܗ ܘܡܕܝܢܬܐ ܕܐܬܚܪܒܬ ܗܘܬ ܠܥܠܬܐ ܕܐܒܠܐ ܠܐܪܥܐ. ܘܡܠܬܐ ܡܪܝܪܬܐ ܚܕܬܐ ܡܠܝܬ ܡܪܝܪܘܬܐ ܠܐܕܢܐ ܕܐܬܡܚܝ ܫܡܥܗܝܢ ܡܢ ܡܠܬܐ ܕܗܟܢܐ ܡܪܝܪܐ. ܟܡܐ ܝܬܝܪܐܝܬ ܗܘܬ ܡܪܝܪܘܬܐ ܐܠܘ ܕܐܬܩܪܒ ܗܘܐ ܚܘܪܒܐ ܘܐܬܚܙܝ
They themselves went out to form a procession, for they set out for the underworld. Happy is the dead man who died shortly before this anger, for tears were shed by his friends. By his bearers, with honor, he was led to the tomb, and he left the city in peace. He did not see the cruel horror, did not hear the violent earthquake. The gravediggers, who buried him and returned, went back in and were suffocated in their dwellings. They buried the dead and returned to be buried in the squares. The city, ancient (and) secular, honored and buried its friends. Of their life, it was the nurse, and of their death, the gravedigger. 151-190. God turned his gaze from the city.
ܕܐܦ ܡܢ ܫܡܥܗ ܫܟܚ ܕܢܥܩ ܠܗܠܝܢ ܕܪܚܝܩܝܢ. ܐܝܟܢܐ ܘܟܡܐ ܥܩ ܠܗܠܝܢ ܕܩܪܝܒܝܢ ܗܘܘ. ܙܕܩ ܕܢܡܘܬ ܚܕ ܐܚܐ ܡܢ ܥܣܪܐ ܐܚܝܢ ܘܣܦܩ ܠܡܥܩܘ ܠܐܒܗܝܗܘܢ. ܘܐܘܒܕܢܐ ܕܚܕ ܩܢܝܢܐ ܡܥܝܩ ܠܗܘ ܕܐܝܬ ܠܗ ܩܢܝܢܐ. ܟܡܐ ܝܬܝܪܐܝܬ ܡܪܝܪ ܐܒܠܐ ܕܟܠܗܝܢ ܐܬܬܘܕܥܝ ܐܬܬܘܕܥܝ. ܕܠܐ ܐܫܬܚܪ ܕܢܒܝܐ ܘܠܐ ܐܫܬܚܪ ܕܢܒܝܐ. ܕܟܠܗܝܢ ܐܬܬܘܕܥܝ
The sign of the Power from above abandoned the cities and they perished. This sign sustains all creatures, without sustaining them with hands. They are suspended from it, without being chained to it. It holds them, without tiring. God, it is not with effort that, like us, he lifts something. It would be weakness in the Almighty if, with effort, he lifted something. It is not after having approached and touched that he can then lift something. The will, which is weak in us, is powerful in God.


[p. 104] {#po.37.p104}
ܠܐ ܡܬܚܫܒܢ ܒܘܝܐܐ. ܕܟܠܗܝܢ ܐܬܚܪܒܝ ܠܐ ܡܫܟܚ ܕܢܒܝܐ. ܫܓܝܫܝܢ ܒܗ ܚܘܪ̈ܒܐ ܫܓܝܫܝܢ ܒܗ ܫܡܥܐ. ܡܛܠ ܕܫܓܝܫܐ ܡܠܬܐ
For the will of God is power; it carries everything. It is a creative arm that does everything without tiring. Man makes his power seen, and God, his will.


[p. 105] {#po.37.p105}
ܫܓܝܫܐ ܐܦ ܝܠܠܬܐ. ܟܡܐ ܡܪܝܪ ܐܒܠܐ ܡܛܠ ܕܩܫܝܐ ܗܘ. ܕܗܟܢܐ ܩܫܐ ܕܠܐ ܡܫܟܚ ܦܘܡܐ ܕܢܡܠܠ ܚܘܪ̈ܒܐ ܕܐܬܥܒܕܘ ܒܗ. ܐܝܢܐ ܟܪܝܗܐ ܕܢܦܩ ܡܢ ܟܘܪܗܢܐ ܐܘܣܦ ܚܕܘܬܐ ܒܚܘܪܒܐ ܗܢܐ. ܕܡܛܠ ܕܡܛܝ ܠܬܪܥܐ ܕܡܘܬܐ ܒܪܘܓܙܗ ܐܠܨ ܣܦܘܬܗ ܘܐܡܪ ܘܝ. ܠܐ ܚܕܝ ܚܕܝܐ ܒܚܕܘܬܗ ܘܠܐ ܚܠܝܡܐ ܒܚܠܝܡܘܬܗ.
Man acts with his arms, and God with a peaceful sign. The sign that envelops everything abandoned the cities, and they were overturned as at the origins. When the Creator created, with a sign he gave life to creatures, through the one who commands, his Beloved. Likewise, with a peaceful sign, he destroys, makes creatures disappear, so that we may understand, through these (events) of the middle (of time), the stories of the origins and the end.


[p. 106] {#po.37.p106}
ܘܝܐ ܗܘܐ ܓܢܝܙܐ ܡܛܠ ܕܡܘܬܐ ܗܘܐ ܓܢܝܙܐ. ܕܡܥܝܐ ܕܐܬܬܙܝܥܘ ܩܕܡ ܝܘܡܐ ܗܢܐ ܕܡܥܝܐ ܗܘܘ ܕܢܒܝܘܬܐ. ܟܕ ܒܟܝܢ ܗܘܘ ܥܠ ܡܝܬܐ ܥܠ ܢܦܫܗܘܢ ܒܟܝܢ ܗܘܘ ܒܒܟܝܐ. ܢܦܫܗܘܢ ܥܠ ܢܦܫܗܘܢ ܕܠܐ ܝܕܥܝܢ ܒܟܘ ܩܕܡܝܬ ܡܣܟܢܐ. ܦܘܡܝܗܘܢ ܥܠ ܢܦܫܗܘܢ ܝܠܠܘ ܩܠܝܗܘܢ ܥܠܝܗܘܢ ܒܟܘ.
Those who perished unexpectedly show, regarding the origins, that creatures existed unexpectedly, as Moses wrote with the help of the Spirit. They also show, regarding the end, that creatures will disappear unexpectedly. The city that disappeared unexpectedly is a mirror of the sovereign Creator. See his sign regarding it: how he destroys creatures. 191-244. Let man look and draw the lesson.
ܙܕܩܐܝܬ ܕܝܢ ܐܬܥܒܕ ܒܟܝܐ ܐܠܐ ܐܝܟ ܪܡܙܐ ܟܣܝܐ. ܕܐܝܟ ܪܡܙܐ ܒܚܝܝܗܘܢ ܒܟܘ ܩܕܡܝܬ ܡܘܬܗܘܢ. ܟܕ ܒܟܝܢ ܗܘܘ ܥܠ ܡܝܬܝܗܘܢ ܡܘܬܗܘܢ ܩܪܒ ܠܡܐܬܐ.
Rise, stand near the destroyed city: from afar see its destruction, like someone who looks from nearby, for the spirit can approach from all sides. Even when someone stands there, if his spirit is not present


[p. 107] {#po.37.p107}
ܥܡ ܩܠܐ ܕܐܒܠܐ ܥܠ ܡܝܬܐ ܐܬܐ ܚܘܪܒܐ ܕܚܝܐ. ܩܠܐ ܕܐܒܠܐ ܥܠ ܡܝܬܝܗܘܢ ܫܡܥ ܡܘܬܗܘܢ.
his presence is distant, for the spirit is not present. He can therefore, even from afar, be present through his spirit.
ܢܒܝܐ ܗܘܐ ܩܠܝܠ ܩܕܡ ܙܘܥܐ ܕܐܬܥܒܕ ܩܠܝܠ ܒܬܪܗ. ܘܐܦ ܡܢ ܝܘܡܐ ܗܢܐ ܝܬܝܪܝܢ ܗܘܘ ܡܝܬܐ ܡܢ ܝܘܡܬܐ ܐܚܪ̈ܢܐ. ܕܩܕܡܝܬ ܐܬܪܫܡܘ ܡܬܠܐ ܒܝܕ ܗܠܝܢ ܕܡܬܚܙܝܢ. ܕܒܝܕ ܝܬܝܪܘܬܐ ܕܡܝܬܝܗܘܢ ܢܬܪܫܡ ܪܡܙܐ ܕܡܘܬܗܘܢ. ܘܒܝܕ ܝܬܝܪܘܬܐ ܕܩܠܐ ܕܐܒܠܐ ܬܒܟܐ ܡܕܝܢܬܐ ܥܠ ܢܦܫܗ. ܡܐܡܪܐ ܫܒܝܥܝܐ ܥܠ ܢܝܩܘܡܝܕܝܐ ܐܒܠܐ ܥܠ ܡܕܝܢܬܐ ܚܪܝܒܬܐ ܡܕܝܢܬܐ ܕܐܬܚܪܒܬ ܗܘܬ ܡܒܘܥܐ ܕܐܒܠܐ ܠܐܪܥܐ
Rise and look toward it, with the hidden eye of the spirit. See the wise: they were suffocated; therefore seek the wisdom from above. See the rich: they were buried; therefore seek true wealth. See the beautiful things: they have perished; therefore seek the beauty that never withers. See the young girls in putrefaction; therefore always despise the desire of the flesh. See the constructions that have collapsed; therefore build yourself constructions in the heavens. See childhood: it has been carried away; therefore do not worry about old age.


[p. 108] {#po.37.p108}
ܫܡܥܐ ܚܕܬܐ ܡܪܝܪܐ ܡܠܝܗܝ ܡܪܝܪܘܬܐ ܠܐܕܢܐ ܕܐܝܠܝܢ ܕܐܬܬܥܝܩܘ ܫܡܥܝܗܘܢ ܡܢ ܫܡܥܐ ܕܐܒܠܐ ܗܢܐ ܟܡܐ ܝܬܝܪܐܝܬ ܐܠܘ ܗܘܐ ܩܪܝܒܐ ܕܐܦ ܠܪܚܝܩܐ ܐܥܝܩ ܫܡܥܗ ܟܡܐ ܘܐܝܟܢܐ ܐܥܝܩ ܠܩܪܝܒܐ ܕܐܢ ܐܚܕ ܡܢ ܥܣܪܐ ܐܚܐ ܢܡܘܬ ܣܦܩ ܠܡܥܩܘ ܠܐܒܗܘܗܝ ܘܐܘܒܕܢܐ ܕܚܕ ܩܢܝܢܐ
See the wealth over there; therefore do not grieve over poverty. See laziness: it has been ruined; therefore be zealous for penance. See the rebels who had not reconciled, and the resentful who had not agreed. Here they are over there, suffocated and abandoned, and their animosity, with them, has gone away. He who distrusts his neighbor does not enter the kingdom.


[p. 109] {#po.37.p109}
ܡܥܝܩ ܠܐܝܠܝܢ ܕܐܝܬ ܠܗܘܢ ܩܢܝܢܐ ܟܡܐ ܝܬܝܪܐܝܬ ܡܪܝܪ ܐܒܠܐ ܕܟܠ ܡܕܡ ܐܬܬܘܒܕ ܒܗ ܘܠܐ ܐܫܬܚܪ ܕܢܒܝܐ ܚܕ ܘܠܐ ܕܢܬܢܚܡ ܚܕ ܒܪ ܢܫܐ ܕܟܠ ܡܕܡ ܐܬܬܘܒܕ ܒܗ ܠܐ ܡܬܚܫܒܢ ܒܗ ܢܘܚܡܐ ܕܟܠ ܡܕܡ ܐܬܚܪܒ ܒܗ
If he does not resemble its inhabitants, he will not be one of its heirs. He who, down here, has in view the love of the children of the kingdom, (he) the kingdom welcomes, for it sees in him the mark of its sons. If you apply yourself to considering the destroyed city thus, from afar it is a land of election.


[p. 110] {#po.37.p110}
ܠܐ ܡܬܚܫܒ ܒܗ ܢܘܚܡܐ ܡܚܠܛܝܢ ܒܗ ܚܘܒܠܐ ܡܚܠܛܝܢ ܒܗ ܫܡܥܐ ܡܛܠ ܕܡܚܠܛܐ ܡܠܬܐ ܡܚܠܛܐ ܐܦ ܝܠܠܬܐ ܟܡܐ ܡܪܝܪ ܐܒܠܐ ܗܢܐ ܡܛܠ ܕܩܫܝܐ ܗܘ ܕܗܟܢܐ ܩܫܐ ܕܠܐ ܡܫܟܚ ܦܘܡܐ ܕܢܡܠܠ ܚܘܪܒܐ ܕܗܘܐ ܒܗ ܐܝܢܐ ܟܪܝܗܐ ܕܢܦܩ ܡܢ ܟܘܪܗܢܐ ܐܘܣܦ ܚܕܘܬܐ ܒܚܘܪܒܐ ܗܢܐ ܡܛܠ ܕܡܛܝ ܠܬܪܥܐ ܕܡܘܬܐ ܒܪܘܓܙܗ ܐܠܨ ܣܦܘܬܗ ܘܐܡܪ ܘܝ ܓܒܪܐ ܚܕܝܐ ܠܐ ܐܬܒܣܡ ܒܚܕܘܬܗ
When one looks from nearby, the eye melts into tears, for this inhabited place has turned into ruins. The heartbeats increase because of the people suffocated and buried. The mouth murmurs lamentations because of the tall people entangled. There, sadness prevails because of the small people. See: all that has been destroyed, and of all that make little account. Detach yourself from everything, and possess the Lord of everything.

### EIGHTH (MEMRA)

### ON THE CITY OF NICOMEDIA AND ITS NEIGHBORS
ܟܠܡܕܡ ܗܘܐ ܒܨܒܝܢܗ ܕܐܠܗܐ ܡܪܝܐ ܐܠܦܐ ܕܐܠܦܝܢ ܐܒܕܘ ܕܨܒܝܢܟ ܗܘܐ ܚܪܝܢܗܘܢ ܒܪܡܙܐ ܙܥܘܪܐ ܕܚܙܬܟ ܐܬܬܙܝܥܘ ܝܡܐ ܘܝܒܫܐ ܐܬܬܙܝܥ ܝܒܫܐ ܠܐ ܢܡܘܣ ܐܬܬܚܪܪ ܝܡܐ ܠܐ ܬܟܣܐ ܐܬܗܦܟܬ ܡܕܝܢܬܐ ܠܐ ܨܒܝܢܗ ܟܠܡܕܡ ܗܘܐ ܒܨܒܝܢܟ ܫܘܦܪܐ ܘܦܘܪܫܢܐ ܐܬܒܛܠܘ
1-8. Everything happened by the will of God. Lord, countless ships have perished, for your will was their end. With a brief sign of your gaze, sea and continent were overturned. The continent trembled in an unusual way, the sea withdrew in an abnormal way. The city was overturned against its will; everything happened by your will. 9-35. Beauty and particularities have disappeared.


[p. 111] {#po.37.p111}
ܦܩܘܕܬܟ ܚܪܒܬ ܦܪܨܘܦܐ ܕܚܘܒܟ ܫܦܪ ܐܢܘܢ ܒܚܘܒܐ ܛܝܒܘܬܟ ܫܦܪܬ ܐܢܘܢ ܒܕܝܢܐ ܙܕܝܩܘܬܟ ܫܚܬܬ ܐܢܘܢ ܦܪܝܫܐܝܬ ܥܠܘ ܠܐܪܥܐ ܟܠܗܘܢ ܚܕܐܝܬ ܥܠܘ ܠܫܝܘܠ ܡܢ ܒܪ̈ܝܬܐ ܥܠܘ ܚܕ ܚܕ ܒܚܕܐ ܐܘܪܚܐ ܢܦܩܘ ܡܢܗܝܢ ܝܘܡ ܒܝܘܡ ܝܠܕ ܐܢܘܢ ܒܚܕ ܪܦܦܐ ܫܩܠ ܐܢܘܢ ܫܢܝܢ ܥܠܘ ܒܒܪ̈ܝܬܐ
Your order ravaged the faces that your love had adorned. With love, your grace had adorned them; with reason, your justice altered them. They had entered separately on earth; they all entered together into the underworld. Among the creatures they had entered one by one; they left them by a single path. Day after day he had birthed them; in an instant he carried them away. For years they had entered among the creatures; in the blink of an eye they left them.
ܒܚܕ ܪܦܦܐ ܢܦܩܘ ܡܢܗܝܢ ܫܦܪ ܐܢܘܢ ܒܩܘܡܬܗܘܢ ܦܠܓ ܠܗܘܢ ܦܪܨܘܦܐ ܚܡܬܐ ܫܚܬܬ ܩܘܡܬܗܘܢ ܡܘܬܐ ܚܪܒ ܦܪܨܘܦܝܗܘܢ ܕܡܘܬܐ ܡܫܚܠܦܬܐ ܐܬܬܫܦܪܘ
He had adorned them by (their) statures; he had distributed features to them. Anger struck down their statures; death ravaged their figures. With varied resemblances had been adorned the faces that perished there.


[p. 112] {#po.37.p112}


[p. 113] {#po.37.p113}
ܦܪܨܘܦܐ ܕܐܒܕܘ ܬܡܢ ܒܪܘܝܐ ܦܪܫ ܚܙܘܝܗܘܢ ܡܘܬܐ ܐܫܘܝ ܦܪܨܘܦܝܗܘܢ ܒܬܘܩܢܐ ܦܘܪ̈ܫܢܐ ܒܚܒܠܐ ܫܘܝܘܬܐ ܒܬܘܩܢܐ ܬܘܩܢܐ ܚܒܠܐ ܚܘܠܛܐ ܘܚܒܠܐ ܐܝܬ ܠܗ ܫܘܠܛܢܐ ܒܨܒܝܢܗ
The creator had differentiated (their) aspects; death made (their) features similar. In their arrangement, differences; but in (their) destruction, resemblances. Regarding the formation, arrangement; but destruction is confusion and corruption. He has the power by his will to differentiate them and to make them equal. 35-68. Uniformity in the underworld.
ܕܢܦܪܫ ܐܢܘܢ ܘܕܢܫܘܐ ܐܢܘܢ ܫܘܝܘܬܐ ܒܫܝܘܠ ܒܚܝ̈ܐ ܦܪܫ ܐܢܘܢ ܘܒܡܝ̈ܬܐ ܐܫܘܝ ܐܢܘܢ ܦܪܫ ܐܢܘܢ ܒܦܪܨܘܦܝܗܘܢ ܘܫܚܠܦ ܐܢܘܢ ܒܩܠܐ ܕܢܦܪܫ ܐܕܢܐ ܐܝܟ ܥܝܢܐ ܩܠܐ ܐܝܟ ܦܪܨܘܦܐ ܒܚܝ̈ܐ ܐܝܬ ܦܘܪ̈ܫܢܐ ܕܠܐ ܢܗܘܐ ܚܘܠܛܐ ܒܫܝܘܠ ܐܝܬ ܫܘܝܘܬܐ ܕܠܐ ܐܝܬ ܬܡܢ ܦܘܪܫܢܐ ܚܘܠܛܐ ܐܝܬ ܒܫܝܘܠ ܕܠܐ ܡܬܦܪܫ ܚܙܘܐ ܡܢ ܚܙܘܐ ܘܠܐ ܦܪܨܘܦܐ ܡܢ ܦܪܨܘܦܐ ܘܠܐ ܨܠܡܐ ܡܢ ܨܠܡܐ ܡܝ̈ܬܐ ܠܐ ܒܥܝܢ ܡܝ̈ܬܐ
Among the living he differentiated them, and in the midst of the dead he made them equal. He differentiated them by their figures, and diversified them by their voices, in order to differentiate the ear like the eye, the voices like the features. Among the living there are differences, so that there is no confusion. In the underworld there is equality, for there one does not need to discern. There is confusion in the underworld, for an appearance is not distinguished from an appearance, nor faces from faces, nor silhouette from silhouette. The dead are not in search of the dead, that they might need to discern, that a man might know who is his neighbor, and that he might distinguish what is his silhouette.


[p. 114] {#po.37.p114}


[p. 115] {#po.37.p115}
ܕܢܗܘܐ ܠܗܘܢ ܦܘܪܫܢܐ ܕܢܕܥ ܒܪܢܫܐ ܐܝܢܘ ܚܒܪܗ ܘܕܢܦܪܫ ܐܝܢܘ ܨܠܡܗ ܟܕ ܥܝܪ ܘܚܙܐ ܢܘܗܪܐ ܗܝܕܝܢ ܡܬܒܥܝܢ ܦܘܪ̈ܫܢܐ ܕܢܦܪܫ ܚܙܘܐ ܕܢܘܟܪ̈ܝܐ ܘܕܢܕܥ ܨܠܡܐ ܕܝܕ̈ܝܥܐ ܚܙܝ ܒܫܢܬܐ ܘܒܚܫܘܟܐ ܠܐ ܡܬܒܥܝܢ ܦܘܪ̈ܫܢܐ
When one is awake and sees the light, that is when differences are necessary, to distinguish the aspect of strangers and recognize the silhouette of familiars. See: in sleep and darkness, there is no need for differences. How much less, in the underworld, is there a need for differences between silhouettes and figures,
ܟܡܐ ܝܬܝܪ ܒܫܝܘܠ ܠܐ ܡܬܒܥܝܢ ܦܘܪ̈ܫܢܐ ܕܨܠܡܐ ܘܕܦܪܨܘܦܐ ܕܦܓܪܐ ܩܐܡ ܒܡܗܠܟܬܗ ܘܢܦܫܐ ܒܛܠܐ ܡܢ ܙܘܥܗ ܟܕ ܡܬܝܗܒܝܢ ܠܗܘܢ ܚܝ̈ܐ ܗܝܕܝܢ ܩܢܝܢ ܦܘܪ̈ܫܢܝܗܘܢ ܒܪܝܟ ܨܒܝܢܐ ܕܨܪ ܐܢܘܢ ܘܒܒܪܝܬܐ ܒܕܪ ܐܢܘܢ
for the body stops in its walk and the soul ceases its movement. It is when life is given to them that they acquire their differences.
